ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e253	At the request of the Control and Constitutional Committee, the President will propose that the debate be organised in the usual manner, except that the number of lines is limited up to seven lines after each post. – This is deemed to be adopted.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e258	The background to this case is the Riksrevision's study of the State's work on CO2 management. An overall committee agrees that we need to set bold targets in climate policy, and that the development of new technologies is a key instrument in climate policy, both nationally and internationally. There was also some background for the Riksrevision to start their studies of the country’s work on CO2 management. The first objective of the studies was to assess how the Ministry of Oil and Energy ensured that the Storting's objective of CO2 capture and storage was achieved. The aim was also to shed light on the use of resources and progress in projects. The survey covers the period 2006 to 2012. Over the period 2007–2012, the country has spent 7.4 billion. kr for the CO2 management projects. The government has spent about 5 billion. kr for the planning and execution of the test centre, and nearly 2 billion have been carried out. kr for the planning of full scale CO2 capture and storage. In this calculation, therefore, the costs of full scale are not up to the termination and subsequent costs. Those costs will only be incurred until 2012. This is big sums. The National Audit Office pointed out in its report that the high costs represented a risk that the return on investment would not be proportionate to the economic effort. But even I think it's worth spending a lot of money if you're really trying to achieve the goal first, and especially if you're actually reaching the goal. It is small sums to consider the seriousness of climate change. But it's huge sums of money if it stays standing like a waste of public money. In this light, the National Audit Office's investigation revealed a number of alarming findings. In particular, the Riksrevision found that there have been few tools for good cost management and rapid progress in the fishing projects in Mongstad. It is also alarming that concluded agreements have limited the country's governance and that it has been challenging for Gassnova to take care of the country's interests. These are very strong facts that you can't talk away from. There has been a thorough examination of the Mongstad project in the Committee. In addition to the inspection to Mongstad, the Committee held three days of inspection hearings. A large public gallery has been invited and participated in the hearings. I refer to the setting, where there is a chronological overview of who participated. The Committee decided on some overall issues to be taken into account for the hearings. The issues are presented, but among the main questions were: ‘Is there any aspect of the country’s handling of full-scale capture and storage of CO2 that has been cumbersome? Did the Government of Stoltenberg II take the necessary steps – legal, economic and political – to ensure that full-scale CO2 installations could be realised?’ Even I believe this has been one of the core elements of the committee's studies. The majority's comments on this matter are from a solid, large and broad majority consisting of five of the eight parties of the Grand Duchy: Left, Right, Advance Party, Christian People's Party and the Green Party. There is no time frame to give the whole background to the Mongstad case. Then the lines had to be withdrawn to the Bondevik I administration and the start of the gas power case. But in order to understand the close relationship in the Mongstad case, we must at least return to the then Prime Minister Jens Stoltenberg, who in his New Year speech to the Norwegian people in 2007 said the following: ‘Norway assumes a pioneer role when we have decided that the gas power plant in Mongstad will have the greenhouse gas CO2 clean-up. We shall make this possible." Then followed the reference to President Kennedy in the speech, and how Stoltenberg and his government would succeed in what was supposed to be our nation's, Norway's, moon landing. It did not take long since 2007, and especially after the scrapping and during the hearing, before Stoltenberg in the media tried to relay history completely differently, as if the moon landing was not the cleaning of the gas power plant at Mongstad, the full-scale project, and as if the moon landing had actually happened to the test centre. It is therefore necessary to remember the first two sentences of the New Year – and I quote again: ‘Norway assumes a pioneer role when we have decided that the gas power plant in Mongstad will have the greenhouse gas CO2 clean-up. We shall make this possible." This should be our nation's moon landing, no doubt. Purification of the gas power plant with full-scale capture and storage was the objective itself. Therefore, it does not serve former Prime Minister Stoltenberg specifically, or others, to honour trying to twist history afterwards. The majority in the committee considered the resignation of the Prime Minister to the Norwegian people in the New Year's Eve to be unusually clear and expected the red-green government to do what it could to fulfill what was promised. This has not happened. On the contrary, they went back on the promise, changed the strategy, and left the pioneer role on the road, until the whole thing crashed in September 2013. The strategy and focus for Mongstad ranged from the ‘fastest possible’ realization of capture and storage to the ‘costiest’ and ended with ‘no clean-up’. And we can see that the moon landing project was stopped only after it was clear that the red-green parties had lost the election in 2013, but before the government change had taken place. The criticism in the National Audit Office's report and the reference to the weaknesses in the implementation of the CO2 capture and storage project at Mongstad are neither new nor surprising. Most of the weaknesses identified by the National Audit Office have been pointed out and discussed in the Storting several times in recent years. I am therefore not familiar with the statements made by former Prime Minister Jens Stoltenberg in the Committee's hearing on unanimity and broad support in the Stortinget for development and progress in the Mongstad project. The most central instrument for ensuring progress in the full-scale capture and storage of CO2 at Mongstad was the emission permit granted to the CHP plant. We made that clear from the Left when the agreement came up in Stortingen and the permit was to be granted. The State's own competent authority in the field, the State's pollution monitoring body, now the Environment Directorate, recommended, in line with the Left, in 2006, to impose full-scale capture and storage requirements for CO2 from the first day. The reason given was that otherwise there would be a real possibility that CO2 capture would not come into place as intended. It is easy to see that this was also good advice that the government should have followed, but that it chose to skepticate. A requirement for full scale clean-up from the day one would give the right incentives to realise the project. Unfortunately, the Ministry of the Environment and the Swedish Environment Councils chose to disregard this professional recommendation. The Mongstad case has been politically controversial since its inception in 2006. The full-scale capture and storage schedule for CO2 in Mongstad was a central part of the government’s political compromise that resulted in Statoil’s concession to build the CHP plant in 2006 without cleaning from day one. The 2006 Implementation Agreement was contrary to the Soria Moria Declaration, which was the basis for the red-green government cooperation in the autumn of 2005, and went across SV’s demand for all new gas power plants to be cleaned from day one. The very detailed emissions permit from the Ministry of the Environment should be a guarantee that clean-up would come in line with the set timetable. The central importance of the emissions permit for the project was underlined when the implementation agreement was presented to the Storting, where it stated: ‘The Environment Ministry’s emissions permit of 12 October 2006 and the implementation agreement between the State and Statoil on CO2 management at Mongstad form the starting point for the construction of CO2 capture installations at Mongstad.’ The decision of the government of Stoltenberg to postpone the investment decision for the full-scale plant in May 2010 created a heated debate both in the public and in the Stortinget. A motion of censure was made when an overall opposition felt that the then oil and energy minister, Terje Riis-Johansen, had failed to comply with the information obligation to the Stortinget in the run-up to the government's decision to postpone the investment decision for the full-scale project. The opposition considered that the Stortinget had not been particularly informed of serious professional counter-conceptions to the government’s timetable for and implementation of the full-scale project. This was a matter that the government had been made aware of through a number of reports from Gassnova from 28 January 2009 until the postponement decision was taken over one year later. Also at that time, there was an intermediate choice between the time when the government became aware that the project had to be postponed and the time when the postponement decision was formally taken by the government and published. The Mistletoe proposal was promoted by an overall opposition in the context of the review of the revised budget for 2010. Furthermore, the government's management of the emissions permit for the CHP plant in Mongstad has been the subject of repeated debate in Stortinget. It is therefore not right, as Stoltenberg has said, that there has been unanimity and broad support in the Stortinget for development and progress in the Mongstad project. In connection with the emissions permit, I will also refer to Erik Solheim's letter to the Energy and Environment Committee of 12 May 2010, where he writes: ‘The government wants to implement full scale CO2 management in Mongstad, and it is therefore not appropriate to withdraw the emissions permit. Conditions and deadlines in an emission permit under the pollution law may be amended if this is deemed necessary when framework conditions and assumptions change.’;» Such signals undermine the credibility of Norwegian environmental authorities. The terms of the emissions permit, which would ensure the progress of full-scale clean-up operations and which would be risked behind the mirror if the parties to the agreement did not comply with their obligations, were made a pragmatic inventory of responsibilities that could be changed in the event of changes in framework conditions or assumptions. Consistent enforcement of the emissions permit is in reality the only credible instrument left by the government to ensure full scale clean-up in Mongstad. It is worth noting that, although the emissions permit for the cogeneration unit states that it is to be facilitated for the capture and storage of CO2, it was not done. This would entail additional costs to build the capture plant because one had to rebuild the cogeneration plant. This was a further weakness in the implementation agreement. Gassnova said during the hearing: ‘Based on a technical, industrial, non-profit-making situation, combined power plants and capture plants would be planned’. In contrast, Jens Stoltenberg said in the hearing that full-scale cleaning from day one would ‘expose the CHP plant for many, many years’, and that Statoil ‘could not accept such an agreement’. This shows that the capture and storage of CO2 was not the only objective of the agreement, but that it was equally important to allow Statoil to build the CHP plant in Mongstad. Finally, with regard to the ratio of the emissions permit, I would like to point out that the Riksrevision report and the committee hearings have shown that the government makes little use of the terms of the emissions permit to ensure progress in the field of capture and storage. The real medium of the press hollowed out the government itself, which is to criticise. It has been repeated, and will probably also be in the debate today, that the Stortinge has voted in favour of the full-scale plant in Mongstad through all the proposals and budgets that the government has submitted. I have to link some comments to everything now. From the time the Mongstad CHP concession was granted and the implementation agreement was concluded in 2006, the government asked for alternative projects for full-scale CO2 capture and storage. For example, I can. point out that, in the context of the 2007 Implementation Agreement, the Stortinget gave the Government the following instructions: ‘The Committee asks the Government to facilitate the development of further CO2 management projects, where technology development, demonstration and qualification of Norwegian technology can start rapidly.’ Also in Report. St. 9 for 2010–2011, Full-scale CO2 management, it was stated that Gassnova would carry out an investigation work aimed at contributing to a broad and up-to-date monitoring of the scope for full-scale capture and storage of CO2 beyond the project in Mongstad, the so-called CCS capability study. The results of this work were to be part of the government's work on the climate communication and the government's work on CO2 capture and storage. However, the work on the CCS capability study was delayed and therefore did not form part of the government’s work on the climate notification, as the government announced in the Report. St. 9 for 2010–2011. The size, through the treatment of the 2012 Climate Communication, therefore gave the government a mandate to extend and finalise the CSS capability study, so that it would be possible to decide on current projects for full-scale CO2 capture and storage beyond Mongstad. I note that the government of Stoltenberg did not come back to Stortinget at any time with an investigation into alternative projects for full-scale CO2 capture and storage beyond Mongstad – despite repeated requests from the Stortinget to carry out such an investigation in the years 2007-2013, despite the fact that the Stortinget's ambition to realise at least one full-scale CO2 capture and storage facility by 2020 was based on the assumption that such an investigation would be carried out and submitted to Stortinget as soon as possible. In addition, I would like to clarify, on behalf of the Left, that we thought that a cogeneration plant would not be built on Mongstad without simultaneous cleaning from day one. I would also like to make it clear that the Left already in the 2013 state budget went against further transfers to the full-scale project in Mongstad, since it was already clear at that time which way this would go. The left-hand side thought that it was better to look at alternative projects, as has been shown to be correct. I spend so much time on this because, in the light of the information provided by the government, the Storting shared the goal of achieving full-scale installations by 2020, and supported the government in this, neither can nor should it be used by the red-green politicians as evidence that there have been no critical objections to the project. It's on the verge of deliberate attempted history falsification. And it's been tried by some representatives. In his hearing with Jens Stoltenberg, he justified the completion of the full-scale project at Mongstad as follows: ‘There are several reasons why we chose not to continue cleaning the emissions from the CHP plant at Mongstad. Firstly, costs would be significantly higher than previously estimated. When the project was first launched, the preliminary, uncertain, rough estimate was that it would cost about 5 billion. kr. Now the estimate is close to 25 billion. kr. Secondly, the emissions from the installation are significantly lower than previously estimated. Originally, emissions were about 1 million tonnes of CO2 per year. Now emissions are about half a million tonnes of CO2 per year — i.e. whereas, while costs are fivefold, emissions are halved. This means that the environmental benefit in relation to costs has been greatly reduced.’ Information provided, including: from Gassnova, during the Committee's further hearings, revealed major weaknesses in Stoltenberg's explanation, particularly in terms of costs. Former Chairman Johan Nic. In the same hearing, violence expressed the following opinion on the estimate of between 20 and 25 billion. kr: ‘It was extremely high in relation to other types of projects that we had knowledge of at the time. This led to a work process, coordination and streamlining of the project, with the commitment that Statoil had received in the emissions permit. Thus, in effect, the 25 billion disappeared. kr, in my opinion». And he went on to say, ‘We really do not yet know what the final cost would be, because now it was a job towards 2014, the so-called DG2, where to get the first new budget allocation, and towards 2016 when to make a decision. But we don't have these numbers, just to clarify the 25 billion. I'm sorry, I'm sorry, I'm sorry, I'm sorry, I'm sorry. It is only to be noted that former Prime Minister Jens Stoltenberg, in the hearing of the Committee, as the reason for the government's decision to stop the full-scale plant in Mongstad, operated with a cost estimate which, according to the state's own company, Gassnova, no longer exists. The amount of 25 billion. kr, to which Stoltenberg referred, came first as a rough estimate for project design from Statoil's master plan in 2009. Then included the amount of CO2 capture from the two turbines of the CHP plant and from the cracker at the refinery, in total more than 2 million tonnes of CO2. In Report. St. 9 for 2010–2011 remained the same, but the content changed to reduced CO2 capture installations and storage costs. I also find that the red-green government chose to stop the full-scale project only a few months before the next decision time, also called DG2. At this stage, a new and up-to-date cost estimate based on the project design that was then being worked on, a cost estimate that is reason to believe would be much lower than what Jens Stoltenberg operated on in the hearing. I have also noticed that the red-green government chose not to involve Gassnova in the decision to stop the full-scale project. The current Chairman of Gassnova, Einar Steensnæs, confirmed in the Committee's hearing that Gassnova was never asked by the Ministry whether to terminate the full-scale project or whether it should continue. It's very strange, a lot of this, how the outgoing government has been doing. The Red-Green Government decided to publish the decision to close the full-scale project at Mongstad on 20 September 2013. Then a joint press release was sent out by the then oil and energy minister Ola Borten Moe and the then Environment Minister Bård Vegar Solhell, who began with the following section: ‘Collection and storage of CO2 is a central part of the government’s climate action. In order to strengthen the investment, work is now being done on CO2 management. Full scale CO2 capture at Mongstad is phased out and replaced by a CO2 capture and storage programme.’;» Furthermore, as a quote from then Environment Minister Bård Vegar Solhjell, it says: ‘We are now at a crossroads where we have to assess whether the full-scale project should be continued in Mongstad. The Government has come to the conclusion that it is better to open up to other potential projects that can be realised by 2020». In the hearing on 4 February this year, when former Environment Minister Bård Vegar Solhjell was challenged as to whether the press release gave a true and correct picture of the situation, the representative Solhjell replied: ‘I would say that at that time – and now – my assessment, as I have said, was that the chance to succeed in a full-scale project by 2020 and to move on with the Norwegian CCS effort would increase if we outlined the strategy, established a strategy where we tried to find another facility or other methods, find a facility in Norway on which we can have a full-scale scale, and strengthen some other aspects of that commitment.’ When Solhjell was asked what alternative projects he and the government had in mind when the decision was taken to stop full scale at Mongstad, he replied: "I don't want to go into that right now. It was perfectly conscious that we did not submit a project." The solid majority of the Committee, consisting of five out of eight parties, considers the press release of the then state councils Ola Borten Moe and Bård Vegar Solhjell to be highly misleading and suitable for misleading readers of the facts of the case. The government of Stoltenberg’s work with full-scale CO2 capture and storage ended with complete disaster both at Mongstad, Kårstø and other possible sites. And there was not a single concrete alternative project for full-scale installations when the government left the offices. It is certainly the case that the two state councils, Ola Borten Moe and Solhjell, have now left the attempt to produce the final scrapping of the full-scale plant in Mongstad as a strengthening of the commitment to the capture and storage of CO2, is critical. I will also link some comments to openness and transparency. It has been difficult to get an overview and an overview of the case complex. I understand that part of the project contains commercial, confidential information that is not desirable for the public. In matters of such great public interest and which cost so much, I must nevertheless stress that publicity and transparency are particularly important, and that the state must endeavour to maximise public awareness. Although information on the cost of the country has been available, figures have not been transparent and verifiable. For example, in this context, it may be pointed out that until the hearing of the Committee there was uncertainty about the estimate of costs of between 20 and 25 billion. kr. This sum first appeared in Statoil’s Master Plan in 2009 and has been the current cost estimate until the end of the project in 2013. However, the actual measures for which it is calculated in the estimate have changed over time, so that crackers, one or two gas turbines and the question of transport and storage have at different times been out or within the cost estimate. Greater transparency and clarity around such elementary parts of such a comprehensive and technically complex project is essential for controlling progress by the Storting. This is particularly true of a project of such public interest and launched as a measure to clean Norway’s largest greenhouse gas industry. It must be expected that the voters will be able to follow the basic progress more closely than has been the case for the capture and storage of CO2 in Mongstad. I think the government should have given a more realistic account of the project's future after the cost estimate of 25 billion. kr was found in Statoil’s master plan in 2009. The project, and the objective of cutting Norwegian greenhouse gas emissions, would have benefited from a real debate on how to significantly reduce costs and that decisions on the future of the project were taken earlier. It is also worth noting that there has been no independent quality assurance of estimates and plans. The majority of the Committee questions whether the actual decision on the scrapping of the Mongstad project had been taken before the revised budget 2013. In the revised budget in 2013, the government of the United Kingdom announced that they would devote more money to the full-scale project. Reference to Prop. 149 S for 2012–2013, where it reads: ‘On this basis, the funds for the full scale project are proposed to be increased by a net of 35 million. $806 million. $841 million. crowns in 2013.’» In this context, I will refer to Innst. S. No 210 for 2002-2003, where a unanimous Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs decided on the disclosure requirement: ‘The Committee would argue that the government must be obliged to make serious professional counter-representations appear in the case even if they are contrary to the Government’s proposal. Furthermore, the government must be responsible for disclosing whether uncertainty information is involved.’;» And on the other hand, ‘The Committee will also underline the government’s obligation to provide important information that it receives after the issue has been brought before the Storting.’ Neither the former Prime Minister Stoltenberg nor the former State Councils meeting in the Committee's hearings have provided information that would require the government to actually decide to complete the full-scale project in Mongstad at a much earlier date than in September 2013. This together with the absence of new information on the project itself after the 2013 revised budget – which neither Stoltenberg nor previous government councils could provide any response to in the hearing – questions the actual decision of the outgoing government. I will also be allowed to link some comments to the Technology Center in Mongstad. The Technology Centre shall contribute to technological development for increased global deployment of CO2 capture installations. The aim is to qualify and test technologies, gain experience and contribute to the dissemination of CO2 capture and storage solutions. The National Audit Office points out in its report that the test centre has been realised with a high level of ambition and high standards, but that high costs may reduce interest in increased industrial participation. The National Audit Office therefore recommends ensuring the long-term use of the test centre through, for example, the Ministry. to consider further measures to reduce operating costs and to motivate wider industrial participation. I look forward to up-to-date information on the work to reduce the operating costs of the TCM in future budget proposals. I would like to conclude by saying something about the way forward. Although the full-scale project was complex, the original objectives for full-scale carbon capture and storage at Mongstad were realistic. But it's quite clear that the Storting's original goal of full-scale capture and storage of CO2 in Mongstad, known as the moon landing, has not been achieved. A project that was supposed to capture and store up to 2 million tonnes of CO2 per year has not been able to store any CO2 at all. I would like to point out that an overall committee has noticed that the Riksrevision believes that a clarification of the legal and organisational framework for the transport and storage of CO2 is a prerequisite for putting in place the full scale of the capture and storage project. The National Audit Office recommends that the Ministry strengthen its efforts to establish a legal and commercial framework for the transport and storage of CO2. The Committee agrees and expects the Ministry to follow up this recommendation. The Committee also considers that although the Storting has joined the government’s proposal to scrape the Mongstad project, strengthening the state’s efforts to control costs and making effective progress on any new project is crucial. It is an important task for Norway to contribute to reducing costs related to the capture and storage of CO2 from industry and fossil power generation, but the majority of the Committee believes that there should be a completely different process for the selection of full-scale sewage treatment plants in Norway. The majority, consisting of members from the Right, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party, the Green Party and the Left, have come to the conclusion, after an overall overall assessment, where a number of points and conditions have been pointed out, that it is necessary and appropriate to propose to the Stortinget to take decisions on criticism. There are several issues isolated that alone are enough to express criticism of the outgoing government's work on CO2 management. The report of the National Audit Office is in itself a powerful diet, and five out of eight large-scale parties believe that all these issues together are so serious and serious that the majority of the Committee – all except the representatives of the Labour Party, the Centre Party and the SV, belonging to the previous government – considers it necessary to submit proposals to the Stortingen to take decisions on such criticism. Finally, I would therefore like to recommend the proposal for a decision which the majority is behind.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e717	In my opinion, the launch of a large national carbon capture and storage programme in 2006 was a correct and very important decision. It was courageous and it was visionary, and it was right of the then Prime Minister Stoltenberg in his New Year's speech to give it the name «our moon landing». This is the kind of decision that is policy at its best. It was a decision supported by a comprehensive Norwegian political environment, and of which a comprehensive Norwegian political environment should be proud, and it is a decision that has been confirmed by a unanimous increase in all important crossroads. The challenges of this case are therefore our common challenges. But it is not such to understand that the government is not responsible as the executive government – responsible to Stortinget. The reports that came at that time, that is, in 2006, both from the United Nations and from others, demonstrated more than ever what man-made climate change does to our planet: the sea level is rising, the police are melting, and we know that the poorest people in the world experience extreme drought and long flood periods, which take them away, among other things. the possibility of food production and clean water. Unfortunately, this has only been reinforced in the years that have passed, and no later than Monday, we were reminded that climate change will reduce food supply, with the risk of new conflicts and fundamental differences in the world community. In our own country too, the consequences of climate change are felt by the fact that we have had a lot of weather: strong storms, floods, early spring, drought or very much rain. I don't think there are any Nordics who don't understand that climate change will affect the whole world, including us, as the leader of the UN Climate Panel recently said – or rather repeated. A major cause of climate change is emissions from major power plants, from large industrial plants and from transport. This was a starting point for the bold political decision taken in 2006. Emissions from coal-fired power plants are increasing. Nearly two thirds of the growth in emissions is due to increased use of coal, and once a power plant is built, it must be very much until it is shut down again. This was also a starting point for the decision taken in 2006. There was no simple solution that was chosen at the time. Mongstad was a complicated place to work with the capture and storage of CO2 because it was to be retrofitted to a power plant that was already in operation, to an active refinery, where costs are high, and to a place where it is full of industrial activity. Nevertheless, the government of Stoltenberg felt that it was realistic, and the Stortinget felt that it was realistic – but certainly with many uncertainties. In 2005 and 2006, most people believed in higher, and high, international CO2 prices and that the technology for the capture and storage of CO2 was more mature than it has proved to be in the past. One of the questions that need to be answered is this: Could not the government, despite high cost estimates, have continued to expand in Mongstad? The answer to that is, as I see it: Yes, it would be possible to build a full-scale sewage treatment plant on Mongstad, but the question is at what risk, at what price and what would have been demonstrated to the rest of the world. What we would demonstrate here in Norway is that we would have been willing to use very large resources to clean a relatively limited amount of CO2 and that this technology is extremely expensive for the rest of the world. The world would not find it very attractive to follow in Norway’s footsteps with such a result. From the start of the project, costs were doubled and emissions halved. The heat demand at the refinery was reduced and thus also the production at the heat plant. In Report. St. 9 for 2010–2011, the government writes: ‘Full scale CO2 management in Mongstad will limit Norwegian greenhouse gas emissions. An equally important objective of Norwegian technology and competence development is its application outside Norway. Only then will large reductions in greenhouse gas emissions be achieved as a result of the Norwegian initiative.’ The work on CO2 management at Mongstad has dealt with two different projects: firstly, the construction of a technology centre, and secondly, the cleaning of the emissions from the heat and power plant at Mongstad. The centre was opened in the summer of 2012 and was built within adopted budgetary frameworks. It is the world's largest CO2 capture technology testing plant; both gas and coal cleaning technology can be tested there. The National Audit Office then also determines that the installation has a high potential to contribute to relevant technological development. This is something we need more of. As a result of political decisions and political implementation, we can state that we have gained new knowledge, we have developed technologies, and the world's largest testing laboratory for CO2 capture technologies is at Mongstad. Then, of course, there is still reason to be disappointed that the goal of full-scale cleaning in Mongstad was not achieved. I share that disappointment. It is also important to stress that the end of Mongstad for the previous government was also about setting a new strategy – and not least about maintaining the goal of full-scale clean-up by 2020. The plan was to provide new estimates for the summer. This work was naturally completed in connection with the government’s decision to complete the full-scale work last autumn. I record that the Stortinget has not done so." And on the other hand, ‘And there is no basis for decision. The problem with the project reserve was not only that the Storting was not familiar with it, but that it also became self-fulfilling, because it was included in the basis for the project’s calculation – and therefore for the distribution of risk among the actors. We remember all this unfortunate distribution, which meant that planning was the responsibility of the state. As a result, Statoil was given very good incentives — quite natural, commercially considered — to spend the most of the costs and to spend the most of the time planning, so that the transition to a period of responsibility would take place as late as possible. You can see that there is also no good initiative to get ahead of the project. This lowers the financial risk for industrial partners. Thus, the financing model has given industrial partners weaker incentives to limit costs. ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e1177	The case of the National Audit Office’s study of the state’s work on CO2 management mainly concerns CO2 management in Mongstad, but some attention has also been paid to CO2 management in the gas power plant at Kårstø. The survey period is 2006–2012, i.e. in the period of the resignation of the red-green government. In order to understand the government’s starting point in CO2 management, it is useful to go back to the 2005 Soria Moria Declaration of the Red-Green Government, where government parties say, among other things.\t\t You can change the settings of the grid. \t\t \t ‘The government’s objective is to become a world leader in the environmentally friendly use of gas.’ Furthermore, it is stated that: ‘Natural power has started the expansion of the gas power plant at Kårstø. The government will ensure that work is started on establishing a full scale CO2 removal plant on Kårstø and will contribute financially to this. The aim is to remove CO2 by 2009 so that it can be used as a pressure support for fields in the North Sea. If it becomes appropriate to apply the gas power station concessions granted to Forests and Kollsnes, the Government will also contribute to CO2 removal by them.’;» The Government Platform further states: ‘that research into environmentally friendly gas power is increased and that the innovation company Gassnova is allocated sufficient funds for this purpose’. This clearly shows that the government had great ambitions for the capture and storage of CO2, and during the treatment of the 2006 State budget, a target was set to capture and store CO2 from the gas power plant at Kårstø by 2009. Since one here had only four years available from the ambitions were expressed to the removal of CO2 would be a reality, it is obvious that the government had believed that there was technology available to capture CO2 from the exhaust gases at the gas power plant from day one. With only four years at its disposal, the construction work itself had to start immediately if the objective of removing CO2 in 2009 were to be a reality. Named after, for example: The investigation by Norway’s watercourse and energy directorate, NVE, concluded that a sound and ambitious timetable until its start in the turn of the year 2011/2012. This means that the level of ambition of the government was recommended to be extended by 2 to 3 years compared to the original. During the hearings held by the Control and Constitutional Committee, questions were asked to the former Council of State, Åslaug Haga, about purification by 2009 was more of an intention to do so than reality. To this Haga said: "I think there's two things to say about it. First of all, this was a period when it was made clear in the international climate-affected environment that without CCS, the two-degree target was out of reach. There was a pretty big ‘hype’ around this at the time, and there was an optimism about the possibilities of finding solutions and like ‘crack the coconut’ on CCS that was big, right? In retrospect, it is easy to see that we had too much faith that technology was readily available, but we were not the only ones in Europe and around the globe – probably – who at that time believed too much that technology was right around the corner.’ This seems to be an honest answer, but it is not acceptable for one to initiate large projects at very high costs, on the basis that the CCS environment was ‘hype’ at that time. It was also clear in 2006 that the handling of CO2 from a gas power plant was not profitable. The Ministry of Oil and Energy therefore concluded that the construction and operation of the capture plant and a solution for the transport and storage of CO2 as a whole had to be financed by the State. CO2 capture planning at Kårstø has cost 381 million in the period 2007–2011. kr without the realization of the construction of capture, transport or storage facilities. In the context of cost assessments for the Kårstø plant, planning costs are estimated from 330 to 357 million. kr, while the investment costs, made over different periods of time, range from 3.5 to 15 to 33 billion. kr. After spending 381 million. kr at Kårstø, the project was scrapped. What have we learned from the investigations at Kårstø? According to Gassnova's board, the experience of Kårstø was used in a dialogue with Statoil to reduce the costs of the full-scale project in Mongstad. However, this did not give results in terms of significantly reduced costs. Gassnova learned a lot from the Kårstø project that might have been exploited even more strongly. Mongstad thermal power plant was not part of the Soria Moria declaration, but became a case in 2006, when Statoil said they had to upgrade the refinery, among others. to strengthen the power supply. This created hard fighting inside the red-green government. The working party was on Statoil's side and could not accept that the refinery was closed. Finally, there was a compromise in the government that said yes to gas power, but also called for CO2 capture and storage by 2014. When Stortingen dealt with the project, it had two parts: test out technology at test centre build full-scale cleaning by 2014 In the Stortinget debate on Mongstad in May 2009, the red-greens were clear that the 2014 requirement for clean-up was absolute and that full-scale cleaning was more important than the completion of the test centre. In the debate on 7 May 2009, Mr Erling Sande from the Central Party said, inter alia: the following: ‘It is important to stress that the test centre is not connected to the full scale system in such a way that it must be completed, the tests carried out and the evaluation completed before proceeding with the establishment of the full scale system. On the contrary, the Government's plans to set up a full-scale facility are fixed in 2014. This is important to clarify in the light of the uncertainty that one has tried to address this issue in the later years.’ So the Mongstad project was full-scale cleaning, not just the construction of test facilities. It is clear that the capture and storage of CO2 was very central to the government as former Prime Minister Jens Stoltenberg used the ‘moon landing’ metaphor on greenhouse gas cleaning CO2 and he stated the following in connection with his new year in 2007: «We must take our responsibility. Climate emissions need to be reduced. Norway assumes a pioneering role when we have decided that the gas power plant in Mongstad will have a clean-up of the greenhouse gas CO2. We shall make this possible." And on the other hand, ‘Our vision is that within 7 years we will put in place the technology that will enable greenhouse gas emissions to be cleaned. It will be an important breakthrough to bring down emissions in Norway, and when we succeed, I think the world will follow. This is a big project for the country. It's our moon landing." It's very good that we set ourselves bold political goals, but when we use the metaphor "moon landing", most of us think back to when President John F. Kennedy of the United States in 1961 agreed to set a goal of putting a man on the moon before the decade was over, and bring him back safely. In 1969 the Americans were able to put a human on the moon and bring it back to earth. The moon landing was a fact only eight years after the vision was pronounced by the president. When using the metaphor ‘moon landing’, this is such a powerful metaphor that at the same time a high drop height is achieved if one fails in the goal. Yes, a nation provides Norway as such a high height of fall because, in view of what is going to happen, one is largely attracted to the world’s eyes, and the reputation of Norway as a technology nation can get a bad scratch in the paint if one does not succeed. Over the years, the nation of Norway has carried out very large technological achievements, from industrial travel to large hydropower deployments, and in the last part of the last century a huge leap forward in offshore oil extraction, where technology development and research have been given a dominant place to solve the problems. In terms of technology development to solve the task of Mongstad, i.e. carbon capture and storage, it is critical that one has not used the opportunity to involve our research institutions to a greater extent than what has been done. On questions to Nils A. Smoking in SINTEF, on research bodies such as SINTEF/NTNU has been involved in the CCS project, replied: ‘Not in the strategic assessments, but we have been involved in specific projects, for example: to find out what are the levels of amine emissions, detection methods and that kind of thing. But we have pointed out that Research Norway has not been very well cared for in those discussions." It's not a technology development nation like ours that we don't use our famous research institutes as contributors when technologies are to be developed and tested, and I hope that a learner will learn from this and include them in new big projects. On 20 September 2013, the previous government decided to close the full-scale project at Mongstad. In a press release by the Minister for Oil and Energy and the Minister for the Environment, it is said, among other things, that:\t\t You can change the settings of the grid. \t\t \t ‘Collection and storage of CO2 is a central part of the government’s climate action. In order to strengthen the investment, work is now being done on CO2 management. Full scale CO2 capture at Mongstad is phased out and replaced by a CO2 capture and storage programme.’;» It is almost incredible that from the Council of State it is supposed to claim that the government has CO2 capture and storage as a central element of climate action, while at the same time shutting down the project that could deliver large greenhouse gas cuts. Yes, the amount of CO2 available from Mongstad is approximately. 2 million tonnes and is probably the largest point emission we have land-based in Norway. It is therefore not very credible that we should scrape this project, while catching and storing is a central part of the government's climate action. This is reinforced when Jens Stoltenberg, during the hearing, says the following: ‘The Technology Centre of Mongstad is fully operational. The Centre and the comprehensive research programmes make Norway a leading nation in providing the world with one of the most important tools for global climate change. This vision, this new crime in the development of new and untapped technologies, with which we are in the process, still deserves the term'moon landing'. As a nation, we must continue to set bold targets in climate policy. The climate problem requires political courage and willingness to bet.’ It is critical that our former prime minister claims that the government has done new work with new and untapped technology. We have been told from the hearings that both amine technology and carbon technology are known technologies from many years back. It is also critical to claim that the government has solved the problem in Mongstad by claiming that the lunar landing is a fact. It is wrong when Jens Stoltenberg describes the test centre as a moon landing. He's probably pretty far from reaching the moon. However, he has just entered for a fine landing in Brussels – to take it with him. Anyone who has access to the processes in this case and is aware of the premises clearly sees that one has not made a moon landing with this project, rather it can be described as a real crash landing. It is completely incomprehensible that the project was stopped as: Mongstad is the largest point emission in Norway, with 2 million tonnes if all original emission sources are included, including the cracker at the refinery. The size had granted the money for the CCS project. Statoil was ready to continue and would come up with a renewed cost estimate, which it was suggested could be lower than previously outlined. The cancer of amine technology was solved. Technology is considered to be mature. Gassnova was ready to move on and was not informed that the project would be stopped. The outside world looked to Norway to learn from this project. We have an operational test center on the subject. One might be left with the feeling that the essential part of the government has never really wanted a full CO2 management at Mongstad. It seems that the strategy has been that a gradual move from sustaining the political ambition to capture and store CO2 to not doing anything, or saying otherwise: going from the fastest possible realization via cheapest and finally no clearing. One is now left with a test centre that has cost approx. 5 B.C.E. kr, and for which there is some uncertainty about the post-use. However, this shall continue until 2017 and will therefore incur additional large costs during that period. At the same time it is used approx. 2 billion. kr to full scale CO2 capture and storage without taking a single walk in the ground. In addition, a significant sum has been used on Kårstø, which was finally scraped. This result is critical. The five parties Right, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party, the Left and the Green Party are united in taking decisions on criticism in this matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e1524	Today’s debate is based on the National Audit Office’s study on how the Ministry of Oil and Energy ensured the achievement of the Stortinge’s CO2 management target, and not least to explain the reasons for the development of resource use and progress in the CO2 management projects for the period 2006-2012. When the majority in this case concludes that the government's work on full-scale CO2 capture and storage at the Mongstad plant is critical, it is not the first time the gas power case has created large waves in the Stortinget. We from the Christian People's Party think it is right to withdraw the lines until the first time the gas power case was put at the head of the Stortinget in the spring of 2000. It was the time when the centre government of Bondevik I chose to ask cabinet questions to prevent the construction of gaseous power plants in Norway. I think it is interesting to remember that until then, the Workers' Party had accepted the emissions permit as a prerequisite for cleaning any gas power plant on Kårstø, but in the spring of 2000 it was decided to reverse the situation. Instead of continuing to require clean-up, the Workers' Party chose to force a change to the emissions permit by saying yes to the construction of gas power stations without cleaning. The Workers' Party, together with the Right and Advance Party, went so far as to be willing to change the pollution law in order to achieve this. The Workers' Party, under the direction of Jens Stoltenberg, worked hard in Stortinget and chose to overthrow the central government of the gas power plant, and in a day like this it is in its place to remember that the Kårstø power plant without cleaning actually stands as a monument to Stoltenberg's government takeover in the spring of 2000. When the Soria Moria declaration was presented in the autumn of 2005, SV claimed to have won a historic victory, which set a final point for the gas power struggle. The party chose to send out a press release entitled ‘15 years of gas power plant struggle has ended. The environment won!" Half a year later, the winner was lost when the Workers' Party enforced that Statoil was allowed to build gas power plants on Mongstad, starting in 2010, with cleaning only in 2014. SV accepted the agreement, but required verification of the emissions permit from the Ministry of the Environment as just a rice behind the mirror to Statoil. The rest of the story we know too well: the gas power plant in Mongstad is built and operated, while the full-scale capture and storage still only live as a vision. And we know the Riksrevision's verdict on the Mongstad project. The complexity was underestimated. The TCM Centre, with its high costs, creates a reduced interest in increased industrial participation. There are few tools for good cost management and rapid progress, the framework for transport and storage of captured CO2 has not been clarified, and there are weaknesses in the Oil and Energy Ministry’s risk management and Gassnova’s internal control — to mention something. We all also remember the new year of the former Prime Minister Stoltenberg in 2007, referred to by several previous speakers, where the promises of CO2 capture and storage were made, a promise that meant that Norway would create a lunar landing project in CO2 management. A few sentences from there: ‘Our vision is that within 7 years we will put in place the technology that will enable greenhouse gas emissions to be cleaned. It will be an important breakthrough to bring down emissions in Norway, and when we succeed, I think the world will follow. This is a big project for our country. It's our moon landing." This promise to the Norwegian people was unusually clear speech. Expectations were made that the government would do what it could to fulfill what was promised, and then it is strange and disappointing to record that the government chose to go back on the promise, changed the strategy and left the pioneer role on the way, until the entire project crashed in September 2013. There is also reason to stress that the strong criticism of the National Audit Office’s report through the reference to the many weaknesses in the implementation of the CO2 management of the Mongstad project is neither new nor surprising. We in the Christian People's Party do not recognize ourselves in the statements made by former Prime Minister Stoltenberg at the Committee's hearing on 20 January. that there was broad support in the Stortingen for development and progress in the Mongstad project. On the contrary, Stortinget’s treatment of the Mongstad case shows that it has been politically controversial since its inception in 2006. The full-scale CO2 management schedule in Mongstad was a central part of the government’s political compromise that led to Statoil’s concession to build thermal power plants in 2006 without cleaning from day one. And just to underline that cleaning would come into place, in line with the timetable presented, the emissions permit was added to the Ministry of the Environment instead of to the State pollution control, as was done in connection with the gas power plant at Kårstø. We remember all the debate that was created by the government in May 2010. It was decided to postpone the investment decision by, inter alia: the result of a motion of censure against the Minister for Oil and Energy from an overall opposition, as set out by the rapporteur. So it's impossible to establish that there was broad support for development and progress in the Mongstad project. There were several voices, several critical voices. Another important element in understanding what went wrong is the changes to the emissions permit made by the Ministry of the Environment from 2006 to 2010 – the guarantee that the Soria Moria declaration would be honoured. The 2006 emissions permit states, inter alia: in point 2: ‘Full scale CO2 management from the CHP plant shall be established by 2014.’ And then we're going to go to point three. ‘The work of facilitating and implementing capture of CO2 from the cogeneration unit shall start immediately: [[...]] In 2010 it was heard as follows: ‘– The first stage of CO2 capture was to be implemented as quickly as possible and not at the same time as the start-up of the CHP plant. – The requirement to store captured CO2 from Step 1 was removed. – Full scale CO2 management from the CHP plant shall be established as soon as possible within the framework of normal industrial practice.» There can be no doubt that these changes, contrary to the promises made, have contributed – and this is perhaps one of the most serious – to undermining the credibility of environmental authorities. The National Audit Office’s report states that the complexity of the project was underestimated when the implementation agreement was signed in 2006. From the outset, there have been conflicting views between the State and Statoil on how the full-scale project was to be implemented. The government’s vision was to take on the pioneer role and assume responsibility for the realisation of one of the world’s first full-scale CO2 capture and storage facilities by 2014, which provided for the use of already existing technologies and limited opportunities for technology development and technology qualification. This was reflected in the Implementation Agreement through clear deadlines for investment decision and completion of the full scale installation and the following conditions of the emissions permit, paragraph 3, from 2006: ‘The work on the organisation and implementation of capture of CO2 from the cogeneration unit shall start immediately after the emission permit has been granted. The realization of the capture plant shall be carried out in parallel with the construction of the cogeneration unit.» Statoil wanted the full-scale project to be carried out in the same way as other major industrial projects, which meant the use of well-tested and mature technology after a thorough technology qualification. This consideration was also reflected in the Implementing Agreement in a formulation that the project should follow so-called normal industrial practices. We have to note that Statoil's preferred method of implementation was the one that finally won at Mongstad – first at the postponement in 2010 and then the government decided to restart the entire project in 2011, when it was decided to launch a three-year run for the technology qualification of four different capture technologies. I believe that the fundamental disagreement about the progress and implementation of the full-scale project, which is integrated into the Implementation Agreement, actually became an important reason for the whole failure. The following opinion by Mr Helge Lund, Head of the Group of Statoil, at its hearing on Monday 20 January 2014 illustrates this: «CCM was a major industrial and technology development project and thus gave us a very great responsibility as a project implementation. Large-scale after-cleaning of CO2 from flue gas was a little-tested technology in 2006. Both the TCM and the CCM had an ambitious schedule, which we have underlined on several occasions, including by signing up to the Master Plan of 2009, and also by different project milestones." And he continues: ‘The catch project in Mongstad has followed our well-established processes and governance documentation for implementation, i.e. implemented in the same way as we carry out other major projects.’ The weaknesses of the Implementation Agreement between Statoil and the State have been repeatedly discussed in the United Kingdom since 2006. The Christian People’s Party already questioned the Storting’s treatment of the 2007 Implementation Agreement on the embedded uncertainty in the agreement on the timing of progress and the parties’ obligation to take an investment decision in 2012. I also refer to Stortingen's treatment of the revised national budget for 2010, where the first postponement of the full-scale project was dealt with. From the adoption of the implementing agreement, it states, inter alia, that the members of the Committee from the Progressive Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party «questions whether the implementation agreement between the State and Statoil on the full-scale clean-up of CO2 emissions in Mongstad contributes to the delivery of a project in budgetary terms and at the agreed time. These members point out that such an implementation agreement is designed, Statoil has no incentive to implement the project cheaply and quickly. These members point out that the incentives in the agreement indicate that Statoil takes very good account of any costs in the project since, with some exceptions, the company will be financially responsible for any excess costs.» I also refer to Stortinget's treatment of St.meld. No 9 for 2010–2011 on full-scale CO2 management, where the Christian People's Party in Innst. 295 S calls for the development of the full-scale CO2 capture agreement two years after it should have been in place. The reason for the delay was obvious: the implementation agreement, as it was designed, provided Statoil with financial incentives to push on the investment decision for Step 2 Full-scale cleaning. The agreement lacks, as pointed out by the Riksrevision, incentives to implement the project on time and cost, an important reason why the investment decision could still be postponed in time. At the same time, it was requested that Statoil's repeated breach of the implementation agreement should not have any consequences for the company. The following two proposals were made in connection with the examination of the case at that time: ‘The Government is asked to renegotiate the 2006 implementation agreement with a new deadline for full scale clean-up and with an incentive structure that contributes to the timely and cost implementation of the project.’ [[...]] And... ‘The Government is asked to ensure that a renegotiated implementation agreement requires the emissions from the Mongstad refinery to be cleaned.’ The proposals for the renegotiation of the implementation agreement, as has been said by this pulpit before today, only the votes of the Christian People's Party and the Left in the Stortinget. In the climate agreement, it was agreed that the full scale plant would be completed by 2020. Ever since the concession to the thermal power plant was granted and the Implementation Agreement was concluded in 2007, Stortinget has asked the government to provide alternative projects for full-scale CO2 capture and storage. The following instructions were given to the Government in the context of the Management of the 2007 Implementation Agreement: ‘The Committee asks the Government to facilitate the development of further CO2 management projects, where technology development, demonstration and qualification of Norwegian technology can start rapidly.’ We later discovered that the government of Stoltenberg did not come back to Stortinget at any time with an investigation of alternative projects for full-scale CO2 capture and storage beyond Mongstad. We had expected the outgoing government to follow up work on alternative projects, so that the Storting's goal of achieving at least one full-scale CO2 capture and storage facility by 2020 could have been achieved. Unfortunately, we have to note that the possibilities and assumptions of the present government to achieve this goal are now becoming very demanding and difficult. So a few words about the way the cutting of the full scale on Mongstad was done. On 20 September 2013, a press release was issued by the then oil and energy minister Ola Borten Moe and the then environmental protection minister Bård Vegar Solhjell. I quote: ‘Full scale CO2 capture at Mongstad is phased out and replaced by a CO2 capture and storage programme.’; Here, the scrapping of the Mongstad project was produced as a new strategy that would strengthen the CO2 management process. I must say that it was with great wonder that we read this message – a press release which, in the view of the Christian People's Party, was misleading and suitable to mislead readers about the facts in the matter. For the fact was that the resigned government's work on full-scale CO2 management had ended up in a disaster both at Mongstad and at Kårstø. There was not a single concrete alternative project for full-scale CO2 management when the government left the offices. To present the final scrapping as a strengthening of the investment in CO2 management, I have to say, appears to be a little credible. And when, in the hearing of the Control Committee on 4 February 2014, Solhell directly asked what alternative projects he had in mind, the answer was: "I don't want to go into that right now. It was perfectly conscious that we did not produce a project. I expect everyone here knows the Gassnova study that works on different projects. Now I understand that the government is fortunately working on a new strategy, and that we will get more information about it in the context of the revised national budget.’ In other words, it is quite obvious that the outgoing government at all did not have alternative plans that could strengthen the full-scale CO2 management effort in line with the Stortinge climate settlement. Unfortunately, the moon landing project by the government of Stoltenberg ended up as a crash landing. One of the most obvious environmental policy commitments from the red-green government to the voters was not met. Despite warnings in the course of the project, in order to organise and organise it – especially by the Christian People's Party, as I have documented – they chose to hold a firm course in relation to an implementation agreement that clearly underestimated the complexity and limitations of governance, and without having a clear organisational and legal framework for the transport and storage of captured CO2. As a result of this disaster, the potential for achieving the climate policy objectives by 2020 has been greatly reduced. The concern of the Christian People's Party – and I am sure that the concern that we have expressed in the course of the process, we share with others here – that the Mongstad project acted as a postponement of fundamental climate responsibility in the Norwegian oil and gas industry, has unfortunately proved to be right. Unfortunately, there is a basis for concluding that the work of the former government on full-scale CO2 capture and storage at the Mongstad plant has been critical. There is now an urgent need to organise new and important interventions, which I see as a major consensus in the hall, so that full-scale demonstration facilities can still be ready by 2020. But the consequence of the Mongstad project is unfortunately that Norwegian climate policy has been set back many years.

The core of the matter is climate; it is that the amount of CO2 in the atmosphere is increasing, which in turn leads to warming and changing the climate. In the United Nations climate report, one has now said that there is better scientific evidence that increased CO2 in the atmosphere is man-made; this is better scientifically documented than that cigarette smoking is harmful to health. I hope that sentence gets attached to everyone, and I noticed with joy what the speaker of the Advancement Party said here today, and that that recognition is now in place with him – very happy. What source of CO2 are we talking about here? Well, we're talking about the combustion of fossil carbon. It's the fossil carbon combustion and the CO2 that's coming, which is the challenge. It is precisely the point of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change that it is man-made. There are people who can slow down the growth, which can reduce the combustion of fossil carbon, as opposed to, for example: that people can do something about that we're now getting increased methane from the fact that the tundra is thawing. Human beings can do little about it, but here we're talking about something human beings can do about it. It's a professional fact that we can't separate the one carbon atom from the two oxygen atoms in the molecule CO2. It can only happen in one way: through photosynthesis. Photosynthesis works in the sea and on land, in animals and plants that are there, animals that live on plants and other organisms. Some thought the vision of moon landing was a technological copy of photosynthesis, and when it was used the term "cleansing of CO2", it was an association that one here was going to face something where one could distinguish the carbon atom from the two oxygen atoms in CO2. That wasn't the point. It's never been the vision. I hope no one lives in that faith. What was the vision of Mongstad, on the other hand, was that we should have the capture and storage of CO2 – CCS – that many have used without really being aware of what it means: Carbon Capture and Storage – carbon capture and storage. That's why I'm hammering on the concept, because it's so crucial. In the case of climate action, we must have three thoughts in our head simultaneously after the assessment of the Centre Party: We need to mitigate the consumption of fossil carbon through economic instruments, charges, for example, and regulation. We need to capture and store CO2 from large exhaust emissions, as we are now discussing. We must promote the production and use of renewable carbon from cod, spruce and thymotry. So we're talking in the third point about what's called the bioeconomy, where biorefineries are a central element, as opposed to fossil carbon, where you have oil refineries. Conceptual use in the past and now in the context of full-scale installations in Mongstad, as mentioned earlier, has been unclear. One has talked about CO2 handling, CO2 cleaning, CO2 capture and storage. It is also said that fossil energy – yes, the point is that it is much more precise ‘fossil carbon’, which can also be used for energy in case of combustion, which then gives exhaust gas. Lack of precision in conceptual use contributes to the failure of the climate debate to deliver better results in Norway. The intention of Mongstad investments and Mongstad plans was good, yes very good. There were bold purposes to that. The test center, TCM, at Mongstad, has done the job. That's good. It's a huge cost and the greatest cost of what we're discussing today. About 5 billion. kr is connected to the test centre. Following the assessment of the Centre Party and the assessment of many other parties, it is important to further develop the work of the Test Centre, in order to increase knowledge so that we can have lower costs, both investment costs and operating costs, at capture and storage facilities, and to improve the quality of the work done – both price and quality. What is serious in the Mongstad case is the CCS plant, capture and storage full scale. It's now scolded – I want to say in this round. Everyone knew that CCS was expensive and difficult. We didn't make it. The centre party «does not believe that the contractual structure, incentives or key actors functioned as intended». Practically speaking, the project manager did not control the value chain, and — in retrospect — the instruments did not function properly. Because what are we really talking about? Well, in my opinion, we are practically talking about establishing an industrial plant – a large, large industrial plant. And what is the raw material in that industrial plant? Well, it's exhaust gas. The raw material is exhaust gas, which should come from two sources. One was the oil refinery, and the other was the gas power plant. The sum was approx. 2 million tonnes. It was an oil refinery, it was a gas power plant with two turbines. Then there was early uncertainty about the economy of the oil refinery and the lifetime of the oil refinery. Could a bet that one got that exhaust from the oil refinery? And then there was also uncertainty about the gas power plant – will there be two turbines, or will there be one, or what will it be? So one didn't control the availability of raw material. And then that's the question of the product. The product was CO2. Whether I am going to use the term ‘price of CO2’, ‘cost of CO2’, will depend on the position I see it from. But it's a product called CO2, and it's at least related to economics. And when the person who was going to build this industrial plant, and not least the one who was going to operate this industrial plant, which was not the state, did not know the supply or the value of the product, you are in trouble. After my assessment, we are here at some of the things that are practically demanding about the case. And as if that weren't enough, it wasn't just to catch it, one should also deposit it. And then someone had to find a place where you had a geological formation that was so that it stayed there and didn't come up again. It was expected that CO2 could be used as a pressure aid and thus have a positive value for oil and gas activities. It was made clear that that relationship was not present. So it was a significant change. And then to the seventh and last, the geological formations. I don't know enough geology to say what it's like, but I notice that in the discussion around Europe and elsewhere, you're very ‘hands on’ on it, namely whether the geological formations are safe, so it doesn't come up. One thing is under normal conditions, something else is related to the geology and the Earth's crust and everything is changing. It is also unclear who would pay the bill, get any profit in operating the plant. In order to reduce anthropogenic emissions of CO2 from fossil carbon, we have to have three thoughts in our head at the same time. One, reducing fossil carbon consumption, two, increasing the production and use of renewable carbon so that these products can replace fossil carbon products, and three, the current points, namely CCS, with capture and storage at high point emissions. It has to be in place, but it's very expensive. Investment is very expensive and it is very expensive to operate, and it is also very energy-intensive. About 10-20 pst. of the energy that we are treating here must be used to operate the plant. It also helps us in the capture and storage of fossil carbon in the form of CO2 to get an increased price of fossil carbon. And cutting CO2 emissions from fossil carbon, CCS, costs a lot for a country, both in a conversion phase and in an operational phase. We need to do something, and the other country Norway can afford to do it, we have good advice. It's a political choice. It's a huge social responsibility because we're facing a paradigm shift that, in our view, needs to be prioritised. That's a big question. And to get the people's support to do something like that, we have to explain it in a way that far more understands than what we've done so far.
