ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e2051	This issue needs to be seen in the light of the fact that the world is facing major challenges in order to manage to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, so that we do not exceed the two-degree target. Power generation and other use of energy based on fossil energy carriers are the main sources of emissions. If energy needs are to be met globally, we will probably not be able to escape fossil energy carriers in the near future. CO2 capture and storage is therefore essential if we are not to have dramatic growth in greenhouse gas emissions far beyond what the climate can withstand. Norway and the world have committed themselves to this. There's some background for the Mongstad project coming. Mongstad was important because there was a breakthrough for political agreement on CO2 capture and storage in Norway. There was no agreement whatsoever about it in advance. It is at least a political consensus that is crucial if we are to move on. Technologically, great progress has been made, and so political progress has been made, so if, after the debate here today, I am a little uncertain as to whether the consensus actually stands. There were large sums of money spent on Mongstad, but compared to a lot of other things, it's not that big. It was used across 7 billion. kr, and 5 brd. kr of them have been used at the test centre. When, on the basis of the majority of the debate, it seems that this money has wasted little of itself, they say that they are critical of the use of money, with the exception of the Technology Centre. It's a recognition of what's achieved there, and I really hope it's a majority for that. Then it's like you have to dare bet big to solve the serious climate problems. Then you can't just go on projects where you're 100 pst. ensuring that targets are met. But the Mongstad bet has until now left itself four important things. We have a test center that stands, that goes, that can and must be used in the future work. We have built up skills, technology development and learning in Norway. It's not irrelevant. The agreement to expand full-scale facilities is – it has been established in the 2014 budget procedure in Stortinget – and it is important to move forward. And there is a political consensus that no new gas power plant will be built without CO2 cleaning. It's now part of the climate agreement. I've looked up in the media today, and there the Oil Directorate shows that they've found even more oil. But then we can wonder if it should be taken up, and then how. I know of both the political forces and the economic forces that want to do so, which may not have CO2 management and climate at the top of their agenda. So I think we'll get enough to do in the future. The starting point here was that SV was quite clear that the removal of CO2 emissions also had to apply to Norwegian gas power plants. They were the strong forces that pressed forward, despite the fact that we then knew about the climate damage. A breakthrough in the idea of CO2 capture and storage has been made deep inside Norway. It didn't come about because there were commercial interests that pushed it out, or because someone saw a future as a technology developer for capture and storage and having an important role for future-oriented solutions to climate problems. CO2 capture and storage was politically driven forward. Hopefully, the political consensus on the capture and storage of CO2 is solid. I'll come back to it a little bit, because I have a lot of magazine back in the story of this matter and looked at what the different parties have meant along the way. It cracks in many directions. In the long term, CO2 management must be cost-effective in order to compete on equal terms with other climate measures. Development projects will be expensive. The development of CO2 capture technologies is demanding and there will always be uncertainty in the development of technologies from the research stage to scale-up to the industrial scale. There have been many challenges with the Mongstad project. The agreement the state made with Statoil was not optimal, and given that Statoil may never have acted to become the leading company for CO2 capture and storage, the job became difficult. Politically, this was a compromise. The decision on the Technology Centre and CO2 capture and storage was the classic compromise that we have seen in Stortinget many times, where the major parties – the Workers' Party, the Advance Party and the Right – have been strong supporters of gas power stations without reservation of capture and storage. The temperature has sometimes been very high. It has, among other things, The representative of the Christian People's Party was up and witnessed. The Bondevik I government actually asked to throw at this question, and SV supported that government's view. SV, the Central Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left have all opposed gas power stations without CO2 management, because climate emissions had to decrease. This compromise was present in the previous government when the matter was negotiated and driven forward. The compromise is also mentioned in today's vote. Reference is made to the importance of achieving two objectives in Mongstad: ‘The importance of facilitating the capture and storage of CO2 at the CHP plant and the upgrading and development of the Mongstad refinery was a large industrial project with ring-effects in the region.’ This compromise lives on between today's government and the partners, the Left and Christian People's Party. Suffering if SV has now managed to do this job, which means that we have established that the principle is firm, I think it is a job to do by sticking to this compromise in the future, especially when new oil extraction opportunities are waving in the distance. Gradually, the vision of those batches that have previously had no concerns about operating on gas power plants without cleaning changes. We can read that in the position from the Energy and Environment Committee to the 2014 budget. This is all evidence that, in the light of our international commitments, we need to reduce CO2 emissions, and Norway will have an ambition to realise at least one full-scale CO2 capture and storage facility by 2020. Everyone agrees on that now. Everyone also acknowledges that the use of capture and storage technologies is demanding, but progress has been made in the development of technology in the last decade on which it can be built. Everyone agrees. It also states that the Committee has noted that the Technology Centre in Mongstad was established with the aim of creating an arena for the long-term targeted development of testing and qualification of CO2 capture technologies. When the government of Stoltenberg decided to complete the planning work on the full-scale project at Mongstad, it of course attracted attention. On the basis of the completion of the project, all parties in the budgetary position – with the exception of the members of the Green Party – say that the development of CO2 management technologies is essential to our objectives, and that the majority supports Norway's ambition to play a leading role in the future. That's good. I'm worried about the consensus ahead and that it can trace off. You can go on and learn from this. Then you have to be 100 pst. set to do what's in your power to do it. Some of the turmoil that arises comes from the fact that the party that did not support this proposal initially is in charge of the ministry that is going to achieve the decisive and difficult goal. The Mongstad project has been complex. Most of the investments that have been made have been made in the test centre – and used. The National Audit Office recommends that the Ministry of Oil and Energy strengthen its efforts to establish legal and commercial framework conditions for CO2 capture and storage. It has to, and the lessons can be many. It is not the use of money in itself that scares, because it will cost money to realise climate goals. But it'll be incredibly much more expensive both in money and in suffering if we don't make it. ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e2297	The chord shift has been completed.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e2301	The role of the National Audit Board and the Audit Board and the Constitutional Committee is to be a follow-up. We are going to place responsibility for how the Storting decision has been followed up, and most importantly, of course, we are going to lay the groundwork for learning from the decisions that have been made, so that we can make better decisions in the future. Therefore, I would first like to say a little bit about how I have understood this matter, after the review that the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs has done, and then I would like to say something about the consequences that I think this should have for the future. We have heard several quotations from the New Year's Year to Prime Minister Jens Stoltenberg in 2007, but it is important to remember that what he said was that 'we decided that the gas power plant in Mongstad should have the greenhouse gas CO2'. The last time, the representative of the Workers' Party, Martin Kolberg, stated that Stoltenberg's wording was not a promise, but a vision. Thus, between these two statements, the life cycle of a project that the Swedish Parliament’s Committee on Control and Constitution – based on the National Audit Office’s report and extensive hearings – has tried to understand, and it has not been that easy. The statements of Stoltenberg and Kolberg are therefore important, for they relate to the core of the matter: What was the real purpose of the Mongstad project? The starting point was at least clear. One was that even in 2007, everyone who wanted to know, knew that global greenhouse gas emissions had to be reduced, and we knew that without changing policies in Norway too, we would not have to do so. We also knew that Statoil wanted a CHP plant on Mongstad, and we knew that many in the Workers' Party and in other parties on the Stortinget wanted Statoil to have that power plant. The gas power debate had been tough for a long time, and the compromise that was being served was clear: the gas power plant was to be built, but the greenhouse gas emissions were to be removed. That was, of course, the way Stoltenberg’s New Year speech was understood by all of Norway. Fear not, said the Prime Minister, we can both continue our oil business and remove the climate-damaging emissions. Telling people that the emissions were to be removed was of course the whole joke. A lot of people knew well what the concept of a moon landing was all about. President Kennedy's promise to place an American on the moon was a promise to do something no one had ever done before, which you didn't know what cost, and which you didn't know what kind of problems would cause, but which you were going to implement anyway, and which you did. So the moon landing speech was in itself really good climate policy from the red-green government. This was exactly what many hoped for: Finally, the government showed the will to really deliver something more than administrative squeaks that climate action is expensive and unprofitable. Finally, we were going to use Norway's great technological skills, great financial resources and great climate policy ambitions to make a big solution. It created optimism, because of the result we were to get, and at least as much because of the political determination and commitment that had finally come into the climate policy. The New Year's Eve of Jens Stoltenberg wouldn't be quite the same if he had said, "People, now we're going to build a dense new gas power plant, and then we're going to see if we're getting some emissions as well, but it's just kind of a vision, right? In addition, it was accompanied by an important and quite consistent industrial reasoning: Norway is a major gas supplier. The gas is important to us, and it is important to the buyers. It is even going to save Europe from the anti-climate coal, and in this context we are ignoring the fact that Norway is also selling coal to the same Europe as we are going to save from coal. In addition, Norway had one of the world’s major gas companies, Statoil, with impressive technological competence — not least for the storage of CO2. Through the Mongstad project, Norway and Statoil would develop a climate policy and industrial columbi egg – a carbon capture technology that would enable Norway to continue as a gas wholesaler, while developing and exporting the gas 100 pst technology. climate-friendly, and which in the next round can contribute to the really great job: catching emissions from the world's coal-fired power plant. This was a strong and lovable argument for the many who like the oil industry, but sometimes it is a little difficult for Norway to talk both loudly about climate policy and to set a high rate of oil as much oil as possible, which produces greenhouse gas emissions. The political impact was also good. There was a broad consensus in Norway that we were now on the way to a solution and that, through bold political commitment and cooperation between politics and the oil sector, there would be a solution. No longer could anyone doubt that the Norwegian oil sector had an equally sincere concern about the climate problems and was at least as concerned about finding solutions as anyone else. So overall, the Mongstad project signaled a bold political commitment and a commitment from the red-green government for which I unquestionably take off my hat. Of course, I also take off my hat for SV, which has a lot of credit for having pushed this through. That's the kind of policy we need. We need the big and brave political projects. It's an honest thing to go on a click, that things get expensive, that technology doesn't work as intended, and that things take time. But that is precisely why the Green Party is strongly critical of the review and hearings of the National Audit Office and the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs, namely that, on very essential points, the bold will that would alerted to implementation was not sufficiently evident. The Mongstad project was both political and technological Norway's sovereign largest climate project. The technical project planning, organisation and implementation, reviewed by the National Audit Office, must be seen in the light of what the Mongstad project should be. In this debate, we have already spent a lot of time on many issues related to information, reporting, cost calculations and decisions, and I will therefore focus on three things: the emissions permit, the overall communication about the project and the decision to wind up the project. The main item is the emission permit. What was then called the State's pollution control authority recommended was that the emissions permit under the Pollution Act should impose full clean-up from day one. That recommendation was based on Statoil’s project application. In this context, the SFT is no one at all. Assessing emissions permits has been their job for 30 years. With a firm and professional use of the Pollution Act, they have cut Norwegian industry's pollution by 90 pst. Former SFT Chief, current Head of the Environment Directorate, Ellen Hambro, made clear in the Committee's hearings on the SFT's recommendation at the time: The emission permit should be required to be fully cleaned for two reasons: Firstly, because Norway should not have new large emission sources. So the SFT took the climate policy and the moon landing promise seriously. Secondly, because the SFT saw a danger that CO2 capture would not be implemented in practice without a clear imposition from day one. SFT knows well how that works, and they know Statoil and the oil sector well. The emissions permit under the Pollution Act was without comparison the most powerful instrument available to the government to ensure that Mongstad cleaning was carried out. The aim of the Pollution Act is to prevent pollution, and it is routinely used in all areas of Norway to stop, cut and regulate pollution. It is therefore interesting that the representative of Kolberg, on a question earlier today, believed that the use of the pollution law was at the very edge of this case. But the government decided not to use this instrument and not to impose CO2 capture. The emissions permit satisfied itself first to require a cut of 100000 tonnes per year, i.e. something more than 5 pst. of the originally expected emissions, and in 2011 this was also deleted, as we know. Why the government didn't use the pollution law for what it's added are among the remaining mysteries in this Mongstad case. We never got any proper explanations in the hearing. It referred to difficult negotiations with Statoil, but in that case it can only be interpreted as granting Statoil the right of reservation in climate policy. There, even before the project itself began, the government of Statoil released the hook and the political cat from the bag. As you can see, the representative of Kolberg is right: what we thought was a promise from the government to cut 1-2 million tonnes of emissions was not meant to be. That was a vision. That's why there's a lot of talk that the Mongstad project's history from the very beginning was a story of a notified breakdown. The next point is about the way the Storting and society were informed about the development of the project. Other parties have spent a lot of time on this in this debate, and it is perhaps right that the government technically complied with its information obligation to Stortinget. What the government obviously did not do, however, was to inform in a way that made it possible for the opinion to follow in the main parts of the decision of the Norway history’s main climate project. Just because the project's political profile was so high, it was reasonable to expect that the government would go far to provide good information. We didn't get that, and I'll draw out just one example: The 2009 Statoil Master Plan operates with an overall price of CO2 capture from the gas power plant and the cracker of 25 billion. kr. The storage of CO2 was not included in that estimate. In the revised State budget for 2010 and again in Meld. St. 9 for 2010–2011 on full-scale CO2 management did not tell the government that the cracker had been removed, but they began to refer no longer to Mongstad, but to the CHP plant in Mongstad. Then, of course, people should know what this was about, that it meant that the cracker had been taken out, but the reality was that the government did not – in clear language – explain what they expected to get for 20-25 billion. kr. So the costs, and what that meant, have continued to create so much confusion that it was going far into the Committee's work on this matter. So the last point I want to point to is the end of the project. During the hearing, we did not get any comprehensible explanation as to why the project was being scrapped. Both the then Prime Minister Jens Stoltenberg and the then Minister for the Environment, Mr Bård Vegar Solhjell referred to the fact that the costs would make the project a demonstration that carbon capture and storage are hopelessly expensive and impracticable. So that's just the The decision in autumn 2013 to stop the project has therefore left behind some sort of fog of speculation and three sad but real questions: When did the government really give up cleaning Mongstad? When did you really decide to scrape the project? Why was it not informed about this earlier? This is not suspicion. This is not a question of people's intentions. It's just a statement that, after all this difficult hearing, we don't understand why this decision was taken and on what basis. The Green Party has been skeptical about the CO2 capture project at Mongstad. We have always thought that Norway should address the fundamental problem, namely oil production and Norwegian oil dependence. We want to start the job now, as we all know we need to start: a planned build-up of a major societal effort to develop the next generation of sustainable Norwegian welfare societies and, in parallel with this, a systematic downsizing of Norwegian oil activities. Therefore, we believe it is wrong that an end-of-pipe solution such as carbon capture and storage should become the major Norwegian climate solution. In addition, we feared that the CCS project at Mongstad would primarily act as a lightning conductor for climate criticism against an oil industry that could still continue on the same course. Like many others, we pointed out that the solution of retrofitting a CCS plant at the Mongstad power plant was an extra expensive and poor solution that made it even more clear that the care for Statoil's power demands was greater than the care for the climate. Unfortunately, we have got this completely right. The Mongstad project has cost Norwegian climate action for eight years at a standstill. Each and every molecule of CO2 from Mongstad is now in the atmosphere – not in space, Tetzschner, it would have been nice if it had ended up there – and it is there to build up the enormous problems that our descendants will inherit. Unfortunately, the Mongstad project has become a monument to a Norwegian climate policy where the majority will not commit to the important, namely cutting emissions. We want to buy emission reductions. We want to research emissions, but we don't want to do it if it's not easy and cheap, and it's not. Was CO2 cleaning at Mongstad too expensive? 25 B.C.E. kr is a lot of money, but it is such sums that Telenor invests and loses on mobile phones in India. Norway has this money – in plenty of monn. Statoil has them himself. They spent 100 billion. kr to buy tar sands in Canada. During the eight years of the Mongstad project, at least 1113 billion were invested in Norway. kr in additional petroleum activities and thus further destruction of the future climate. But 25 billion. So kr was too much to complete what was supposed to be Norway's grandiose and strategic climate project. It's unfortunately pathetic, and it gives ominous signals of further willingness to spend money on climate action. But a project like this, of course, still has a lot of value, first and foremost by learning from it. Firstly, major, courageous, ambitious climate projects in Norway are necessary. The red-green government showed that it was possible to launch it, but we have to learn that we have to commit ourselves to implementing it. Then the most basic assumption, as also shown by the National Audit Office's review, is that we actually have to use the tools we have to ensure implementation. One example is the pollution law. There are many others. Secondly, we need to commit ourselves to a comprehensive climate policy. We must either get a climate law, or a similar mechanism that actually obliges the government to deliver the emission cuts that Stortinget has adopted. If we had, Mongstad would have put us in a completely different situation than we are now, because at the same time as the Mongstad project was terminated, the government would have had to answer where it would then cut the 1-2 million tons of emissions that were not cut in Mongstad. Should they be taken on Snow White? Were they to be taken in car traffic? Were they supposed to be taken on the pedestal? Today, the government has a profoundly embarrassing list from the Environment Directorate of climate action that is not implemented and that totals up to 8 million tonnes by 2020. So Mongstad is just one of them. Neither the previous government nor the current government has any clear plan to solve this problem. The lesson from Mongstad must therefore be that from now on, Norwegian climate policy in general and the use of carbon capture and storage must be focused on one thing, namely reducing physical greenhouse gas emissions in Norway. The hearing made it clear that operational carbon capture and storage technologies are now available. The train has gone too slow without binding CO2 gain on technology development projects. The Green Party still believes that carbon capture and storage are not a main solution, but we must now use it to cut large emissions in Norway. The government should therefore promptly publish commercial offers for CO2 capture and storage with existing technologies on Norway’s large emission sources such as Mongstad, Snow White, Brevik, etc. and with clear requirements for rapid implementation. This is the only way that the bulk of the population can avoid an even more embarrassing accident than Mongstad, which is a disaster for climate reconciliation's very modest cut targets. The Mongstad project must therefore mark the end of an era in which Norway met with visions of a world-leading climate policy. Now it's time to deliver in practice.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e2510	The world's population is increasing, and a greater proportion of the world's population has expectations of stable supply of energy. As a major energy exporter, Norway should contribute to meeting these expectations. At the same time, this increases the need to succeed in decoupling the growth of energy production from the growth of CO2 emissions. A central element of this work is the development of methods to clean the emissions generated. Both the International Energy Agency and the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change point out that the capture and storage of CO2 will be important for meeting the global climate challenges. A prerequisite for achieving targets is that several countries actively focus on technological and competence development, but also on developing projects for CO2 management. The aim must be to increase knowledge and reduce costs. As of today, the technology is too expensive for many to put it into practice. Within the energy field, Norway is in a special position. Not only do we have large oil and gas resources, we also have large renewable hydropower resources. Where other countries have to base large parts of their energy supply on coal, we have hydropower plants that deliver renewable energy without emissions. That's good, also for the climate. At the same time, it means that we have a few large emitters of CO2. But through the petroleum activities and work at Mongstad, we have gained considerable knowledge of CO2 capture and storage. The only full-scale CO2 management projects in operation in Europe are located on a Norwegian base: the Sleipner field in the North Sea and Snow White in the Barents Sea. After years of research and development, through, inter alia: the CLIMIT research programme is the world's leading Norwegian research community on CO2 capture and storage technologies. In addition, the technology centre at Mongstad is an important test centre for testing different technologies for CO2 capture. So to the question whether it's worth the billions that are used, or not. I don't think it's right for me to say anything about that. The attitude of the Control and Constitutional Committee – which is also concerned with the review by the National Audit Office of the government’s carbon capture and storage work during the period 2006 to 2012 – shows, however, that there are different assessments of the results achieved so far. I think it is important to stress that it is not my role as the Council of State to exercise control over the work of the previous government in this area, it is a task of the Storting. Nevertheless, I think it is nice when the chairman of the committee, Kolberg, gives credit for giving us the information we have given. I want to say that for natural reasons it is very descriptive and essentially a compilation of publicly known information. I have tried to stay away from the normative, other than whether the amount of information has been large. I have confirmed this to the Committee that it has, and I believe that it is the Committee and the Stortinget that have to assess whether the quality has been good enough. For me now it is most important to look forward. We must learn as much as possible from the considerable work that has been done. CO2 capture and storage has proved more technologically challenging than initially assumed. At the same time, we have learned that the way projects are organised is crucial for their implementation. It is important to strive for a reasonable distribution of risk between the country and private actors. CO2 management – which I have been quoted earlier in today’s debate – is about developing technology. It involves risk. We always have to be prepared that some choices do not lead to goals, or become more expensive or more complicated than we initially expected. This is now once in the nature of technology development and does not necessarily mean that the investment is wrong or that we should not bet on this type of project. So it's also important to have visions. I'm just trying to stress that it's also important to have a significant degree of reality orientation in the further work. Then it is important that we take systematic lessons from what has been done, and use the knowledge of our own work in Norway and in Europe, but also share the knowledge we have acquired, with others who work with the same type of problems. There is currently no commercial market for CO2 management. Therefore, state involvement in projects is essential. This is true in Norway, and it is true in all other countries that believe that this work is important. At the same time, there is something unusual about a country being so directly and closely involved in technology development projects as has been the case with the carbon capture work at Mongstad. It is a very comprehensive and difficult complex, and implementation is, of course, challenging. CO2 management in Norway has been characterised by high political ambitions. We now see that some of the ambitions have been beyond and proved difficult to deliver on. At the same time, ambitions have had broad support in Stortinget and have apparently continued to do so. The information provided by the Storting has been extensive in the period covered by the Riksrevisionstudies. What Stortinget should have been informed about when it came to the decision of the previous government to wind up the full-scale project in Mongstad is outside the period covered by the National Audit Office's report, and I have not made any assessment of that, as I also said at the hearing. The full-scale project at Mongstad is often compared with successful individual projects abroad. We're going to be glad that a few projects are realized. Equally full, we must note that the development of CO2 management has unfortunately also been more challenging internationally and has gone much slower than expected. The EU had an ambition of 10–12 full-scale projects a few years ago by 2015. No full-scale projects in the EU will be completed next year. Developing technologies that are both reasonable and effective has proved to be more challenging than you originally thought. The financial crisis has led to less risk and capital among potential investors, while the price of emissions of CO2 is very low compared to the cost of clean-up measures. The government is working on a new strategy for the capture and storage of CO2 that can help us towards lower emissions through the combustion of fossil energy sources. In the further work on CO2 management, it is important to focus on following the recommendations of the National Audit Office. I might like to highlight two aspects: both the issues of risk management and the issues of cost management, it is probably important that we focus on further work. The main objective of the strategy must be to develop technology so that CO2 management can become a cost-effective climate action. We have an ambition to put in place at least one full-scale CO2 capture and storage facility by 2020. This is clearly a demanding measure. Experience with Mongstad has shown that developing a good full-scale CO2 management project can be both complicated and expensive. I have said that before – and I want to refer to the representative Hansson when it comes to further work, he said it very well – now is the most important thing we can do with this report and the good work of the committee on the matter, to learn from what has been done. It's the most important thing. And the government is obviously concerned that we have to do a thorough job. Norway must make the best use of the resources we use in CO2 management, and contribute to the great CCS virtue, because this cannot be made a world championship; it must be done as a global virtue. This means that we also have to think of solutions that cross-border. It is essential that Norway’s efforts contribute to technological development and cost reduction. The aim must be to ensure that CO2 management in energy and industrial emissions becomes the rule and not the exception in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e2799	I'll be brief. Much has been said in the debate earlier, and I will not discourage the House from repeating the arguments that have already been put forward. Politicians get criticism every day, both in the debate columns in the newspapers, on television, on radio and from this pulpit. That's good -- it's leading society forward. However, the formal criticism of the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs is not as common. It is the most powerful means of action against a government that has left, and it should say a little about the scale and severity of the matter. It all started with Statoil's desire to build gas power plants in Mongstad. In Mongstad, there has been refinery activity since the late 1970s, with a very energy-intensive production. The advantage of a refinery-related gas power plant is that it is possible to use the heat generated by the power plant so that the operation becomes more efficient both economically and environmentally. Construction started in 2007, and the power plant was in operation late 2010. The whole prerequisite for Statoil to be allowed to build and operate the power plant was that it should be clean-up or capture and storage from the first day, i.e. almost zero CO2 emissions. Meanwhile, Prime Minister Jens Stoltenberg holds his New Year's speech in 2007. There he goes high and says that Norway will develop within seven years a technology that allows the capture and storage of CO2. It's going to be a moon landing of our time. Those are big words, they're obligating words, not least because such a bet at such a cost will be at the expense of other environmental efforts. I'll come back to that. Seven years have passed. The result is a gas power plant pumping out half a million tonnes of CO2 per year, a refinery that continues to pump out one million tonnes of CO2 per year, and 10 billion. treasury bills have been used for investment and operation in Mongstad. Not a single gram of CO2 is yet captured or stored. The Mongstad project consists, as most people know, of two parts: a test centre, where commercial operators can test out technology to make it commercially profitable, as well as full scale capture and storage of CO2 from the power plant and the refinery. The test centre has broad support, and is described by the vast majority in positive order. Nevertheless, the Centre has few concrete results to point to, among other things. in spite of robust trials, the Committee has not been given any clarity as to the number of patents withdrawn as a result of the test centre. The full-scale project is the largest and most extensive of the two. No doubt that's what's the moon landing itself. It is now referred to by many environmental organisations as a disaster, and was thus shuddered after last year's elections. Then we have to ask ourselves, how could it end this way? The simple answer to that is that too many aspects of the project have been managed clumsily, and the will for action has sometimes been half-hearted. For example, Statoil has been responsible for the actual implementation of the project, as part of a compromise, where Statoil was actually given gas power plants to build installations for capture and storage. Statoil is known as an oil company with limited experience in this type of project. Statoil is also, of course, a listed company with many different owners. In reality, the company has one main purpose, and it is to deliver the highest possible dividends to the shareholders. They don't run charity. Statoil's main interest, economically speaking, was to set up a gas power plant in Mongstad, so that refinery operations there became more profitable. When the gas power plant was completed in 2010, the technology for the capture and storage of CO2 was not mature enough to be put into operation. The Stoltenberg solution was then to change the requirement for cleaning from the first day to cleaning as soon as possible – that is, a very non-binding formulation. This happens without correcting with other incentives, so Statoil would still have an incentive to complete the project at a reasonable time at a reasonable price. The result was that Statoil started the gas power plant without cleaning, increased its profitability at the refinery, and re-established with very limited incentives to realize a full-scale plant in Mongstad, as the Riksrevision also emphatically points out. It's the responsibility of the departed government. Let me make it quite clear that I don't think that people or organizations are just governed by what gives reward, on the contrary. I also don't doubt the intentions of Statoil or the resignation of the government in the project, I'm just saying, like the National Audit Office, that incentives work. And I think we're naive if we don't believe that incentives also work on a company like Statoil. During the hearing, I asked several questions to the oil and energy ministers who now resigned about the incentives Statoil had to complete the project at a reasonable time and price. None of them came up with a single one, as is evident in the hearing panel. At the same time, the Riksrevision says that the cost control has been poor. Nor has an external quality assurance of the project been carried out and the costs associated with it, which is very alarming, all the time we are talking about a project that could potentially cost tens of billions of dollars. At the top of it all, Gassnova also did not have sufficient professional competence and capacity. However, one of the most interesting things about the case is how the money has continued to flow into the project despite the fact that someone should have realized that it was very likely that the investment would be lost. The hearing asked Jens Stoltenberg and other former members of the government what caused the full-scale project to be stopped. The answer I thought was very interesting. Unlike what I and some others had expected in advance, they did not get the predictable answer that this was an overall assessment. There were two specific aspects that were pointed out: the price tag and the amount of CO2 to be cleaned. In other words, the price tag per tonne of CO2 was too high. It's a very honest thing. I share the assessments with those who have said that it is problematic if we realize a facility that demonstrates a unit cost that is completely unrealistic, but the problem here is that the amount of CO2 in Mongstad and the price tag has been known for a long time – all since 2009 to be accurate. However, the government did not find any reason to terminate the project until after the 2013 option – four years after this information became known. This information has been digested for four years and concludes that the project was too expensive. In the meantime, billions have been cleared out. Of course, you can only speculate on the reasons why you took so long to conclude what you did. It's used together over 10 billion. SEK in investments in operation in Mongstad. We have received feedback that a good part of the investments made in Mongstad can be useful, but we don't get the fasitite for a long time. Many have referred to Kennedy in this debate. I can imagine doing the same thing. He said in the early 1960s that we're going to land on the moon by the end of this decade – not because it's simple, but because it's hard. It can also be wondered whether this is the reason why the project has gone for four years before using known conditions as a decision to stop it – because it has not been easy to give, but very difficult, to the voters. In the meantime, we can reflect a little bit on what 10 billion. kr can give us in climate context. With today's quota prices in the EU, for example, we can do this. Buying climate allowances for 350 million tonnes of CO2, we can buy climate allowances so that 300 million passenger cars can stand still for one year, or we can buy climate allowances for emissions in Mongstad, both the refinery and the power plant, for over 200 years. Instead, we have temporarily reduced greenhouse gas emissions by zero grams. If the investment makes a profit in the future, an open question remains. When there's so much gap between how much money you've spent and what you initially had, it's critical. That's why we have this debate, and that's why we deliver the matter as we do.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e3058	The representative, Lundtigen, earlier called for some members of the Committee on Energy and the Environment to speak in this debate. I got that with me, and here, of course, I am to link some comments to this discussion. First, I want to commend the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee for the work they have done on the review of the entire Mongstad process. As chairman of the Committee on Energy and the Environment, it is important for me to stress the importance of being able to implement CO2 capture and storage projects. The Intergovernmental Panel report, which was presented last Monday, which shows that climate change is already an international reality, is something we have to deal with when it comes to the changes that are coming. In order to reduce emissions both nationally and internationally, the technology of capture and storage is central to achieving the necessary reductions in the decades ahead. In this way, I also fully agree that it is important to invest a lot in order to get projects with capture and storage in place. As has also been underlined here in this debate, there has been a broad consensus in the Stortningen to bet, and there has also been a broad consensus on using large funds to set up a capture and storage project in Mongstad. But then the reality is that what we're standing back with is a refinery and a thermal power plant where all the emissions are emitted by well over one million tons every year. We're standing back with a test center, which is technologically at the forefront and the largest thing we have internationally, but it's still a question of whether the effect of this will be positive, because you have to have an economy for the big players in order to put this into practice. So even if the technology is there, there's still a big question about whether we want to take the necessary advantage of the test center. Why have we ended up in this situation? It is clear that although there is broad consensus in the Storting, which has supported the objective, there is no doubt that the government has been responsible for the implementation of the “monthly landing” – the development of capture and storage in Mongstad. The National Audit Office has pointed out many mistakes that have been made, and there are aspects of the project that should have been organised differently to get the necessary progress. One of these – and what we have to learn from this project – extends back to when I myself sat up at the gallery here in March 2000 when the debate was about the Bondevik I government, and it finally had to go off because it was not willing to agree to the establishment of gas power plants without capture and storage from day one. I believe that, in order to achieve the necessary progress, it is important to maintain that, when carrying out such large projects, it is necessary to make it a prerequisite for the establishment of capture and storage from day one. In 2006, when working with Mongstad, that assumption was abandoned. It is quite clear that when you are going to establish capture and storage after a project, it is much more difficult to get this in place, and it is also becoming more expensive. Then it is necessary to have good agreements with industry on implementation and agreements that give the right incentives. Here we have had a situation in Mongstad where Statoil has been responsible for the implementation and costs of implementation, while the state has been responsible for all planning. It is clear that when planning costs and their use can cause you to lower the costs of implementation afterwards, incentives are flaws in driving the project forward. It's also been that, despite the fact that the state has spent more than 7 billion in total here. kr, it is Statoil’s quality assurance procedures that have been followed. The lack of external quality assurance at the test centre is quite incomprehensible to me when you are implementing such a large project. In my former position as urban council here in town, both one and two external quality assurances are carried out when setting up a sports facility. When running such large projects, it is clear that this must be in place in the process. As a previous speaker also mentioned, the pollution law and its requirements are effective means of ensuring its implementation. The Mongstad project also shows that changing the permit along the way, or not setting requirements from start-up, works badly. It is important to take us on board, when we are going to have new projects, that the pollution law must be applied, but we also need to stick to the decisions taken throughout the process. The Court of Auditors has some clear recommendations. They have to be reviewed, and they should be followed. The overall risk management needs to be improved in the country's work on CO2 management, ensuring the long-term use of the technology centre through, inter alia. to consider further measures to reduce operating costs and motivate wider industrial participation, to assess measures to strengthen Gassnova’s efforts to carry out cost management and to contribute to effective progress in planning the Mongstad full scale project, and to strengthen efforts to establish legal and commercial framework conditions for transport and storage. Where we are today, this is about the fact that we still need to put in place capture and storage and implement the technology of capture and storage. Mongstad has now fallen away. We need to look at opportunities to put in place the full-scale capture and storage and have this in place before 2020. Without Mongstad, there are not many of the major sources of emissions left in Norway. Here, then, we should be able to look beyond Norway's borders and contribute, while also ensuring that we have technology developments and the positive aspects of implementation. There is considerable responsibility on the part of the Minister for Oil and Energy, but it is also possible to look at the smaller projects in Norway in connection with the upgrading of the Svalbard coal-fired power station and see how we can promote capture and storage with the significant emissions that, after all, are also in the border region. Catch and storage are essential for not only us, but also for achieving climate objectives internationally. In the Mongstad process, in my opinion, implementation has been too bad. The incentives for good implementation have been incorrect. Quality assurance has been lacking, and there is much evidence to suggest that the political performance has exceeded the thoroughness and quality of the planning and implementation phase. If the necessary thoroughness is not present in all terms, it often turns out that the result is too bad – and in this case it has definitely not been too bad, but there has not been any results at all.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e3106	When I put foster homes and child protection on the agenda, it is with the background of the many enquiries and the focus it has been on this important topic. Nevertheless, we have to admit that there is a long way to go until we have a service that works well enough, with good enough quality, enough staffing and enough foster homes. We want all children to grow up in safe, stable environments with good adults in their lives. That's why we have to work together to see if we can find better solutions in the future. First, I want to commend all those who make an effort for the best of their children, who open their home and their lives to children who are not well. There's a lot of respect for that. It is therefore important that they receive the follow-up and support they need, and it should apply to both foster parents and to any child who receives the nurse. That child protection is a co-player, and that everyone is safe on each other, is absolutely necessary to succeed. Often the chemistry doesn't match, and that's exactly why good practice and rules are important. Nearly 1,000 new children each year need foster homes. Many people are in great need of help and need long-term follow-up. Competence and manning are an important factor. This applies to both employees and those who make themselves available for children in crisis. Even with the earmarked funds in the child-restraint lift, more employees and increased competence in child-rescue protection, we are not on target. It must be further strengthened and not stopped. The waiting time for a foster home is too long, and it's increasing. The growing number of children in need of foster homes worries us. There has been a desired twist from institution to foster home, but it will also be the case in the future that some children need institutional space. Therefore, a wide range of services is important in order to meet the different needs. The best thing is to grow up in an almost normal family life, because it provides the best basis for a stable adult life, seen in a life span of 0-22 years. Unfortunately, what we saw in the Focus Programme on foster homes is not an exceptional case, and one can see that recruitment does not get any easier after such an entry. There are wide variations from municipality to municipality in terms of competence, number of employees and the follow-up of cases. There are grey zones between the state and the municipality, and between what Bufetat is going to do and what the municipalities are going to do. It may be a challenge for the same person to investigate, process and then follow up the child and the family. We need trained employees who know their job, and it's important to see if education is adapted to what's expected. There is also reason to look at the practice of putting graduates into the first-line service, where one should perhaps have had them with the most experience and good human knowledge. It must be difficult for employees to face difficult issues throughout the working day, and it is important that they also receive the follow-up and the training they need. These are often people who burn for the best of children, but they burn out because they don't get it. They have too many things. The maximum limit should probably be around twelve, but we see that it's far above what's real. For foster parents, frequent shifts of case handlers are a problem when a person wants to create good cooperation. Feedbacks show that the follow-up fails and creates conflicts. Would it be a solution to have one as an investigator, and one that follows up? In order to recruit and maintain existing foster families, it is important that foster families are confident that they get help when they need it and that they have a co-player in child protection. This suggests that the need for more support and guidance is needed. How can we do that in a better way? One solution might be to develop and use the training programme for foster parents, PRIDE, more. The evaluation of PRIDE shows that the majority of participants gave positive feedback and considered the themes addressed to be useful. What has been mentioned – and should be changed – is, for example: the rights of foster parents and that more realistic images of challenges and of the system should be provided. These are things that should have been inside this program. The further training courses offered have received good feedback and the courses should be developed more. Passing through a course makes foster parents more prepared. The question is how to get even more to participate, or whether one should consider whether at least a basic course should be obligatory. Many children are in a shelter before they go to foster homes. It's the responsibility of the state. When I have promoted this matter, I hear that the contact between the different levels can be difficult. All communication should go through Bufetat, while important information about the time the child has been in the emergency room, which is important for a case handler to know about, should go through Bufetat. It is important for the case handler to know how the children are at home, but there is no direct communication. This may be important to take into account when finding foster homes. This is where children should win their best – over the system. Here, there are great differences between municipalities and municipalities in how Bufetat deals with this. How are we going to be able to do that? Should we have common rules that make sense? Can we learn from Australia and the United States, where one has a demand for close and committed cooperation between agencies? We must have a diversity of offers. Governments themselves have highlighted SOS children’s cities, which are one type of offer. There are the foster parents who move, and the children who live again, if it intersects. There are good meeting places for family meetings in the same area, which I thought seemed very positive. It seems like a nice way to organize it, even if it can't replace all other foster families. The scheme imposes great demands on families who enter, they often state their own lives. Someone sells the owner-occupied dwelling and stays there permanently, while others have a dwelling in addition. It is also relief – well agreed. But this is a scheme that doesn't fit everyone. That's why I want to say that more diversity and good solutions can be a way to go. SOS children's cities seem like a good offer. I look forward to the debate here today, and I think we should work together to find good solutions for children and foster parents. The point of the interpellation is not to say that someone has done a better job than others. Here I think we have a common responsibility to find even better solutions for the kids who are not doing well, for those who want to become foster parents, but who are not thirsty today, and for those who are foster parents, but who want to end because they think it is too difficult. I hope we have a good debate here, and that we can work together to find good solutions.

First, it's about recruiting, second, it's about a good investigation of the child before placement, and third, it's about good follow-up and guidance for foster parents. Last, but not least, it is about the framework conditions of foster parents. It's the children's, youth's and family's budget, Bufetat, who's responsible for recruiting and distributing foster homes. It is important that they recruit different types of foster homes that can care for children with different needs. Foster homes should also reflect the family structures and cultural diversity of society. Therefore, Bufetat has to work specifically to recruit in environments that are underrepresented today. Unfortunately, the waiting time for foster homes has increased. It has, among other things, the relationship with the higher proportion of children placed in foster homes than before. According to Bufetat, there is a need for approx. 1000 new foster homes each year. When placed in foster homes, childcare services shall always assess whether any member of the child’s family or close network can be selected as a foster home. In general, family-based children are better off than other foster children. fewer physical and mental health disorders. Positions also end less frequently with unintended movements than other locations. These foster parents must receive the same follow-up and guidance as other foster homes, and I have asked Bufdir for 2014 in the Children's, Youth and Family Directorate's letter to develop a professional recommendation for assessing the place in foster homes in children's families or close networks. Many municipalities use family councils. Family councils have also been shown to be well suited when placing a child away from home. By means of family counsel, the family may conclude that the child can be placed with a member of the family. That is why I have commissioned Bufdir to stimulate the recruitment of several foster homes in the child's family and close networks through the use of family councils. We know that the most important thing for many children is to have contact with the sibling. I am therefore pleased that we have now clarified in the Child Protection Act that after taking care of the child, the child protection service is to facilitate association with the sibling. Unfortunately, we hear stories that this does not happen, but it is important that it is taken into account. If all those placed in foster homes undergo a thorough pre-location examination, foster parents will also be able to assist the child from the start. A good investigation could help reduce unwanted fractures and movements and provide a more stable and predictable life for the unborn child. It is important that local authorities give priority to this when investigating the child before being placed. What is also crucial for success in foster homes is, as I mentioned, good follow-up and guidance from foster parents. Those who choose to become foster parents undertake a very demanding task on behalf of the public. In order to make foster parents safe in their role, to strengthen their motivation and to reduce the risk of new abortions, it is essential that these foster parents receive knowledge-based guidance. On that occasion, I would like to present a film made by the Adults for Children in cooperation with the National Children's Protection Association. ‘Speak about it’, the movie is called. The aim of the film is to strengthen the relationship between foster children and foster parents, and it should be the starting point for conversation and dialogue. The target population is primarily foster children, foster parents and foster nurses. The key to success with stable foster homes is that foster parents get proper and good follow-up. Both municipal and state child protection are responsible for the training and guidance of foster parents. The responsibility of the State is to provide guidance on whether to be a foster parent, but it is the municipality that is responsible for providing guidance related to the individual child. I have now asked Bufetat to build up an offer of knowledge-based group guidance for all municipal foster homes. It starts this year. Priority should be given to municipalities with the highest needs. The offer shall be free of charge for municipalities and shall also be free of charge for foster parents. It's set at 10 million. kr in the budget for that year. I also asked the directorate to identify and further develop methods that strengthen the link between children and foster families. The monitoring of the financial environment of foster parents and the evaluation of the use of state foster homes and municipally-enhanced foster homes are also important measures, as mentioned in Prop. 106 L for 2012–2013. These measures are well under way, and we will soon have a survey of exactly this from Bufdir. The evaluation of state, municipal and private professional foster homes will be ready by the end of the year, together with Buffdir’s technical recommendation on how to further develop the foster home. These studies will also make a significant contribution in further efforts to ensure better and equal framework conditions for all foster parents and to assess what kind of foster home we will need in the future. The representative Mant pulls out SOS children's cities in his interpellation. The undersigned was visiting Norway's only town of children, located in Bergen. I can really recommend the representative to take a walk, because it is an incredibly great offer. There is also an SOS Children's Village planned here in the East. SOS Children's Village in Bergen is a reinforced foster home. Here, children live in common homes – with one or two foster parents and possibly siblings. Several houses are co-located, so that foster parents always have a safe and good support device around them. And if it turns out that foster parents and children are not "fitting together", it's the foster parents who are moving out, not the children. This is the children's home, and it's completely 'usual home'. We should also remember that four out of five foster parents are satisfied with the support they receive, but we cannot be satisfied with this result. A foster family that is not satisfied is a family too much. Many child protection services are now small and vulnerable. Although for some years we have had a stake in several child protection positions, there are still municipalities with very few child protection employees. For these municipalities it may be difficult to follow up foster homes in a good way. If they also have other tasks, it is clearly a major challenge for those municipalities. The government has also started work on changing today's municipal structure. I believe that child protection is one of the services that will really serve fewer and more robust municipalities. We're dependent on foster parents. Then we have to follow them up. If we don't do that, the kids will be the losers. We can't afford that. The focus programme clearly showed that we have major challenges. When a five-year-old girl has to move up to five times, we haven't succeeded either in caring for that child or in looking at the possibilities of following up foster parents in a good enough way. The representative asks in his interpellation about how we're going to follow up Prop. 106 L for 2012–2013, and she also had a couple of recordings in her post. I would like to say that some municipalities have today shared child protection services in an investigation and an action section. Guidance has also been launched for newly recruited/trained child protection educators – it is important that they receive guidance. It is also important to ensure that municipalities are responsible for controlling the emergency homes for which they are responsible. What's important here is that we always think, what's the best for children? Then we need a diversity, and we need to create safe, good framework conditions around the children who need help.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e3522	Finally, the day we've been looking forward to has arrived. With this Document 8, we see the beginning of a year-long process of abolishing the limitation periods for killing, rape and incest. The road here has been long. Although, in fact, there has been a majority in this case for several years, political insidiousness has made serious crimes obsolete in a row and series. It is very gratifying that support for this legislative change seems to be becoming cross-political. It is equally gratifying that the law change will come quickly. I would like to thank the proposers in the Christian People's Party who have put the matter on the agenda, and the Progress Party for having been interested in this matter for a long time. In particular, there is reason to thank the Council of States, which repeatedly shows that the mantra ‘traffic force’ is more than just a slogan. Removing these limitation periods is a right thing to do. However, from some quarters, it has been argued that at some point all criminal offences must be forgotten. It might apply to some conditions, but certainly not to all. Today, we can get a situation where a killer goes free – not because the evidence is missing, not because the admission is missing, not because the terms of criminality are missing, but simply because a standardised limitation period has accidentally expired. That a killer should be able to avoid liability and judgment because the clock ticks and goes against a statutory deadline, is simply not well enough justified. So it's not like that, as someone claims, that it will now be free to bring back ancient cases for court. It is raised above any doubt, as the representative Ulf Leirstein once said, that matters that are obsolete under the old law will still be. It has also been claimed by some critics that it is resource-efficient for society to make serious crime obsolete at some point in time. This is also a very little striking argument, because it will not be possible for all things now to be taken up – far into eternity. We need to know that evidence loses its weight over time, and that's something both the prosecution and the courts have to take into account in their assessments. There may still be some crimes that go into the history books without judgment, but what we're doing now is removing a square and formalist rule that has stood in the way of completely fundamental principles of justice, and we're replacing them by making it a legal assessment – not a measurement of deadlines. Some never hurt doctors. Even if a judgment itself cannot replace it, it can solve. Although a judgment itself cannot repair, it can relieve. This change in rules, which Stortinget is asking for today, provides an opportunity for more victims and survivors of serious crime to receive the upliftment they had never been able to receive. The right is very much in favour of the proposals made in Document 8.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e3541	First, I must be allowed to thank the case rapporteur from the Progress Party for a good cooperation. And then I have to say that I like the new parliamentary situation, where it is actually such that the Storting is doing a real job, and not least that the Christian People's Party is adopting its proposals. But to the matter: Drap, incest and rape are very terrible and serious crimes that violate the human being most seriously. This is a crime that characterizes the victim and its relatives for a very long time, maybe life out. The rules of limitation restrict the possibility of criminal proceedings and thus also the possibility of the offender being convicted. The reasons for maintaining the rules on limitation in the event of killings and serious infringements are of minor importance in this respect. Enforcement of the law in such cases is more important both for individuals and for society in general. I'm going to take some of the arguments against the decision we're making today. First of all, those who claim that the removal of the limitation period may increase the risk of the murder of a criminal, either by the loss of evidence or by the fact that the evidence becomes more unreliable. Yes, some of the reasons behind the rules are that evidence can become more uncertain the longer it goes from the criminal relationship to the case before the court. The evidence must be clear, regardless of the time – the presumption of innocence is established. And so this argument does not have such a meaning in matters as serious as killing and gross assault. The change we are making in the rules of limitation today does not change the terms of criminality, so the legal certainty of the individual citizen is equally strong – if not stronger. In addition, significant technological progress has been made in recent years, which speaks to re-assess this. When we have the opportunity to find the perpetrator after a long time, through e.g. biological traces, it will be essential that it is also possible to judge the perpetrator. So to the argument that the limitation rules are to ensure that the prosecution authority investigates criminal offences within reasonable limits. We are today deciding to remove the limitation period for killing and sexual assault, and killing and serious assault are to be given priority by the prosecution authority regardless of the rules on limitation, which in my opinion does not allow this argument to be given much weight. A third argument is that the need for punishment decreases over the years. If the guilty person has not committed new offences, the need to punish the offender will be reduced and if the offender has committed new criminal offences, he may be punished for them. This consideration applies in very many cases, but not in cases involving incest, rape and killing. The need for punishment will not diminish in such cases as the proposal refers to, because of the serious nature of the criminal offence. It may be intrusive, both for the culprit and for the family, if the culprit is punished many years after the offence has been committed, but by killing, incest and rape this argument cannot be given the same weight. Let's clearly pick the victim's side. The limitation period for killing and rape should have been removed a long time ago, but today, fortunately, we have a majority of the Christian People's Party's proposals to remove it. It makes this an important day for many and can help people who would have escaped the most serious crimes now be held responsible for their actions, even after a long time.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e3577	The Judicial Committee has now presented a unanimous case to the Stortinget asking the government to promote a legislative amendment to end the limitation period in criminal proceedings relating to incest, rape and murder. The limitation of criminal offences is a fundamental principle in our liberal state of law. Although we have today lifted the limitation period for crimes such as genocide, war crimes, crimes against humanity and terror, we must guard against creating a precedent that lowers the threshold to eliminate the crime of other crimes. Evidence weakens over time, and the risk of misconceptions and murders of the judiciary increases. Just because the requirement of proof must be as strict when it has been a long time, it will be difficult to imagine that it will be appropriate to commit criminal proceedings more than 25 years after a serious act has been committed. As you know, not one technical evidence alone could lead to a conviction. An overall assessment of the evidence must be required to ensure the detection of criminal offences. Although some evidence may be retained, others may have been lost or become unreliable. The need for punishment in society and by offended and relatives is decreasing as time passes. Many offended people will need to put the matter behind them. The penalty will also have little or no effect on the culprit who may be held responsible for a youth sin at the end of a long life, for example. 50 years later. Overall, it must be essential that the objectives of counter-criminal prosecution are strengthened as time goes on and that the danger of harming innocent people cannot overshadow any criminal need in some cases. In the 2013 consultation round, several professional bodies were critical of eradicating the perpetrators of the crimes in question. Some of the hearing bodies also pointed out that some of the proposals were primarily of political significance, more than a real change that would result in more convictions. We cannot let the media control the development of our criminal law and criminal proceedings. The left therefore opposes the proposal of the Judicial Committee, with the exception of II and IV, for which we would like to vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e3812	Let me first thank the interpellant for a good and thorough interpellation. The police represent the security of the citizens, and the question of where the police are going to have their offices increases engagement. I have a great deal of understanding for that. At the same time, the need to make changes to the local structure of the police arises from time to time. Then it is important that the police act follows the driving rules applicable to such processes. It follows from Section 16 of the Police Act that police are to be organised in police districts, and that these should again be divided into county districts and police station districts. It belongs to the King to determine the police district division and the division into the county districts and police station districts. Some places are several janitorial offices and police stations organised on a common holding, also called regions. Such holdings perform some common functions and are managed by one of the officers or police station managers included in the holding. The purpose of the common holdings is, inter alia, to: ensuring the best possible preparedness in the districts and good service arrangements consistent with the working time arrangements. The content of these arrangements varies somewhat, and there may be differing views on how much of the activities of the county offices and police stations can be regionalised without compromising the intention of the law on what should be the responsibility of a county or police station district. The authority to establish common holdings is based on the Ministry. In allocating powers to the Police Directorate of the previous Government in 2010 to determine the administrative structure of the holding, it was assumed by the Ministry that it is the individual borrower who has the professional management of the Länsmannskontoret, while the internal or administrative organisation of the budget, staff responsibilities, business planning, etc. be provided by the lenender who is the manager of the holding. The Ministry's letter to the Directorate also states that, in the event of a change in the structure of the holding, there will be no changes in the boundaries or in the outlying tasks of the service units, and that the officers' offices will remain and carry out the same police and officers' functions as before. The letter further states that the establishment of holdings shall take place in close cooperation with the municipalities concerned and the professional organisations of the establishment. NOU 2013:9, often referred to as the ‘police analysis’, proposes some organisational changes to the police. As you know, it is proposed to reduce the number of police districts, from today's 27 districts. The selection has outlined three models with eight, ten and six police districts, respectively, and the sample has recommended the latter. Furthermore, the sample recommended that the number of service locations be local, i.e. janitorial offices and police stations are being considerably reduced, from around the present day. 350 places of employment. In order to justify the proposal to reduce the number of police districts, the sample points out among others: that today’s police division does not provide the necessary prerequisites for developing specialist functions and capabilities to manage major and serious incidents adequately. In particular, the background to the proposal to reduce the number of local services is that, according to the sample, the current structure does not provide sufficiently effective local police or enough public services locally. Among other things, the selection points to: that many small places of employment with few staff, related to tasks other than core tasks, do not provide effective prevention, good response or effective crime prevention. Many have supported the selection of the need for fewer police districts. Moreover, the consultation of the police analysis shows that there are widely different views on the proposals, both on how many police districts we should have and on whether changes should be made to the local structure. I focus on building a solid foundation for the follow-up of the police analysis and aim to get to the Stortinget with the matter in an appropriate way. In the meantime, until any changes have been adopted and brought into force, the police must, of course, follow the established legislation with regard to changes. I will not rule out that there may be grounds for adjustments in local structures even before decisions on police structures are taken in the Grand Duchy, but the point is that in this context changes must be made in accordance with the legislation. I have made concrete, formal decisions in the Council of State on the merger of the offices of local officials also recently. The most recent happened in February this year, when Stokke and Tjøme Länsmannskontoren and Tønsberg police station were merged. I am aware that the content of the regional schemes has been discussed in a number of places, including: the regional arrangements in Sogn and Fjordane, Sunnmøre, Nord-Trøndelag and Trom police districts. In some places it has been added to a neighbour to operate a lumbar spine office pending a regional scheme and definition of tasks, including: one in Sunnmøre Police District has been waiting to announce the position of locals in Herøy and Sande and Ørskog. The Directorate of Police has informed the Department that it is now aiming to publish the positions of local civil servants during the spring of 2014. Within each police district, the chief of police is responsible for allocating resources in a way that best meets the needs of the population for police services. In other words, resources must be available where they are most needed. However, the police districts cannot reduce the staffing of each county office to such an extent that the office can no longer fulfil the functions required by the law. En route in conversion processes, one may, for shorter periods, allow one lenner to control two lenner districts. In the past, the Ministry has shown in a different context that the Police Directorate assumes that it will be acceptable within a period of approximately one year. Six months. I want to work to have a good neighbourhood police force that will cover the need for police services throughout the country, but the road to a good neighbourhood police force is not necessarily in keeping all parts of today's structure. The central thing must be what the police deliver. I look forward to good discussions on this in the follow-up to the police analysis. In the meantime, the police shall respect the restrictions laid down in law and order. I will ensure that the police districts receive in a short time an up-to-date overview of the provisions applicable to the establishment of regional schemes and any merger of county offices and police stations.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e3997	Children and young people are the most valuable things we have in our society.
