That is why we need to build on the good work done by many institutions and social bodies, while at the same time addressing the challenges we face. I have been following the debate on children who are being subjected to violence or abuse for several years now. Unfortunately, there is a repetition every year at this time that, when the annual reports from the children’s homes arrive, they show that the police spend too much time before children who have been subjected to violence and abuse or have witnessed them are interrogated. Today's legislation requires that the child's hearing be conducted within 14 days of notification. Unfortunately, the rule today is that this deadline is broken rather than kept. The average waiting time is five to six times longer than the deadline. It's serious, it weakens legal certainty, and we also know that children can harm it. I just met a woman who was exposed to a rape that the son witnessed. It took three months for the woman to be questioned, and six months before the son was questioned. In addition, the mother was recommended not to talk to her son about the event, because it could affect his explanation – although we know that it is important for the children to be able to talk about such events in order to get on with it. It was a terrible thing the police didn't do a good enough job. But this is just one of many examples. And we can't have it like that! But that's exactly what we've said every year, and now I and the Christian People's Party expect the new government to act. For these time limits, one of the greatest threats to the legal certainty of children is today. That's why I've set up four areas I have expectations of: The first is better capacity and significantly shorter waiting times; the second is that the new regulation comes into place; the third is that we make national guidelines for children's homes, and the fourth is that we make the medical examinations mandatory. I started talking about waiting time, and I would say a little bit more about capacity. Since the establishment of the first children's home in Bergen, children's homes themselves have done an incredibly good job of facilitating court hearings and medical examinations. At the end of 2012, half of all court hearings were conducted in children’s homes. The new regulation, to which I will come back more, proposes that the general rule should be that the hearings should be conducted at a children's home, but if the consideration of the person to be questioned so requires, the hearing may be conducted elsewhere. The Christian People's Party agrees. In order to strengthen the legal certainty of children, we need to exploit and build on the competences acquired by children’s homes. However, if all child questioning is to be conducted in children's homes, we politicians must be willing to ensure all access to children's homes and in the longer term assess the need for more new children's homes. We also know that more and more people report abuse, and children’s homes report a strong increase in the number of cases. Oslo has said 40 pst. increase, Stavanger reports about approx. The same just this year. This means that it is important that the appropriations for children’s homes are increased. So to the regulation: in 2010, the previous government set up a working group to review the legislation on child questioning and observation and mental retardation. The working group presented its report in October 2012. One of the recommendations of the working group included: a new rule for the hearing of particularly vulnerable persons. The working group's report has been at the hearing, but it has been silent since April 2013, and the children's houses are waiting impatiently for the new regulation to come. I therefore hope that the Minister of Justice will now take action and introduce the new rule on hearings for particularly vulnerable persons. I have been in contact with several children's houses, and what I get feedback about is that they want the prescription to be put in place. An important point on which a change is proposed is who will conduct the hearings. The regulation proposes that the hearing be conducted by a police interviewer who has completed special training in judicial hearings at the Police College. Some have suggested that judges other than the police may be questioned in order to bring down the waiting time for the hearings. I would like to warn you strongly against this, which includes, among other things: The state children's house in Oslo does. It is absolutely central that there is a police interviewer with competence in questioning, because it is those who have competence in how such a hearing should be conducted. The hearing has both an investigative and a proven side. The questioning of children presents special challenges, and we therefore need to facilitate their being done in the best possible way to ensure the legal certainty of children. So to national guidelines: Today, there are quite different practices with regard to the use of children’s homes. It is important that we are clear on what kind of offers young people should be entitled to, and then we need to learn from those who get good results. One of the examples is the children's home in Tromsø. Last year, they arrived at a waiting time of 22 days from notification to judgment. So what's behind the good results from Troms? There is good cooperation between children's homes, investigations and prosecutions, as well as a police officer who believes this to be important and who is asking for results. In 2011, they set up a work committee to look at how they could reduce waiting times. They came up with the following: It was liability powdering. There was no need for clarity between the investigator and the prosecution level in who was responsible for the conduct of a judicial hearing by the deadline. Investigating issues of violence and abuse against children was not a high priority. There were no good enough procedures for recording cases and good enough coordination of investigations. In Trom’s police district, they therefore have a system of 100 p.m. judicial hearing coordinator. position. The coordinator shall take practical steps in all judicial hearings. This system has spread to several police districts, and the Christian People's Party therefore believes that this should be mandatory in all police districts. The system shall ensure that no more than three working days shall elapse from the time the case is registered with the police until the hearing is scheduled. But: This depends on a management that will give priority to these issues. Let's get national guidelines, and let's learn from Trom's police district. The legal certainty of children must be given higher priority! So for medical examination: in all eight out of ten court hearings, children are not offered a forensic examination. Why is that critical? The medical examination reveals medical findings that can be related to the case in question – in other words, evidence can be lost. The study also reveals things that the child often does not remember or would like to tell about in an interview, and the study can therefore lead to that we are told about an infringement. In a discussion post in the Evening Post on 2 March, the Children’s Ombudsman, the police chief in Tromsø and the director of the Court administration wrote that the police in Troms have received several rejections from the hospital when they have asked for forensic examinations. In the worst case, if such studies do not take place, this could lead to children who have been subjected to an attack being not believed and sent back to a potential attacker. Several children’s homes also say that they have to pay to have medical examinations carried out at the children’s homes. That should be unnecessary. Instead, health professionals should be stuck in children’s homes and ensure that all children who tell about strokes or violence must be quickly guaranteed medical examination. ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e4055	I was almost tempted to answer yes and be done with it, but I do not, for it is a very important and serious matter that the representative Ropstad addresses in the interpellation. Preventing and combating violence and sexual abuse against children is a priority area of government. I very much appreciate the representative's commitment, and that this is now going to be discussed quite widely in this interview debate. Violence and sexual assault are serious crimes that can have serious and long-lasting consequences for vulnerable children. It is therefore important that both police and relief services assist vulnerable children and their caregivers in a good manner as soon as possible. Many child questionings are being conducted today at one of the ten children’s homes in the country. The state’s children’s home is established to improve the care of children and young people under the age of 16 and adults with developmental disabilities who are suspected of being subjected to violence and sexual assault. The case must have been reported to the police. The main objective of the establishment of children’s housing is to strengthen the legal certainty of the child. An evaluation of children’s homes in 2012 shows that the model of children’s housing leads to better care and greater security in a very difficult situation. The children thus have better conditions to tell about their experiences during the court hearing. Despite good experience with children’s homes, the waiting time for a court hearing is in many cases too long. Under Section 239 of the Criminal Procedures Act and Section 4 of the Rules of Procedure, children are to be interrogated no later than two weeks after the event has been notified. In practice, this deadline is often broken. For all children’s homes as a whole, the average waiting time from the review to the interview was 51 days in 2013, the waiting time for sexual assaults was 42 days and for violence was 60 days. The ability and capacity of children to remember and the need to complete the case speak for the rapid implementation of the hearings. I want to add that for adult people in a hectic working life, 40–50 days may not be that long, but for a small child 40–50 days is incredibly long. There may be several reasons why the waiting time for a hearing is long. The capacity of the police is a central factor. The number of family violence cases reported has also increased considerably in recent years. At the same time, the number of interrogators may be too low, and those able to conduct interrogations are often also used for other tasks. Furthermore, it is a challenge to address interrogations involving many actors. The fact that child abuse cases are perceived as very demanding for investigators also contributes to very few working on such cases over time. Lack of continuity and high permeability are, unfortunately, the situation in many police districts. The complexity of infringement cases also contributes to the length of the time taken to deal with them. Rules on court hearing and observation govern the questioning of children, mentally retarded persons and other particularly vulnerable persons who are offended or testify to criminal proceedings. Judicial hearings are some form of evidence for use in the main proceedings, so that children do not witness directly in court. The hearings are at the same time part of the investigation. The hearings are conducted by a police investigator led by a judge. In 2010, a Working Party of the Ministry of Justice and Defence was appointed to investigate and propose changes to the legislation on child questioning, mental retardation and other particularly vulnerable persons, known as the Säverud Committee. The working group would, among other things: consider the role and contribution of the Judge in the hearings, the rights of the addressees and the relationship with the EMC, and place a fundamental emphasis on what would be best for the child in his or her judgements. The aim was to establish clear national legislation to facilitate good consultation and the practical and early implementation of the hearings. The report was handed over to the Ministry on 16 October 2012. The committee proposed changes to the Criminal Procedures Act, the Vergemålsgesetz and the Public Prosecutor’s Guide and a whole new regulation on interrogation. Among other things, proposed that the police be given responsibility for the hearings, so as to reduce the number of actors and to facilitate rapid consultation. The sample also proposed that the use of children’s homes be made primarily compulsory and that the age limit for inclusion in the scheme be reduced from 16 years to 15 years. The committee will equate family and moral issues with a new model of questioning and proposes to extend the right to appoint an aid lawyer. The report also proposes several amendments to clarify the regulatory framework and to facilitate the implementation of the hearings. The Severud report was sent at the hearing on 16 April 2013. The Ministry received 46 consultation opinions with comments, several of which were critical of the proposals in the report. Particular attention was paid to the fact that the working group had not discussed some fundamental legal issues sufficiently. The main objections were that the proposals had not been sufficiently assessed against the best and international law of the child in this area. It was also pointed out that the mental retardation situation had not been properly assessed either. Some also pointed out that the consultation model proposed by the working group may be problematic with regard to the rights of the target group under the EMC. The ministry is now following up — and may have done so for too long — the recordings from the hearing and considering whether additional information needs to be collected. It's a work that's prioritised in the future. I do not overlook the fact that this work may result in proposals that deviate so far from the proposals in the report that a new consultation should be carried out. In that case, I will return to Stortinget with this in an appropriate form as soon as possible. In parallel with the follow-up to the Säverud report, a working group has been set up to develop common guidelines for the future operation of children’s housing and to assess the need for supervision. This is done to ensure a coherent and coherent practice and treatment in children’s homes. The working group shall have completed its work during the spring of 2014. However, I would like to stress that a new court hearing regulation and common guidelines for the State Children's House will not only solve the problems of long waiting. One of the main challenges is, as mentioned, capacity challenges in the police. A number of measures have already been taken, and we are continually considering further measures. Among other things, I have asked for a full overview of the number of police officers with this investigative competence. Education capacity has increased significantly, from 18 to 48, which means that more people are trained within this group. At the same time, we know that we have a kind of reserve in some police officers and women who have the skills but do other kinds of work. I think it's important that we now do some hard work to get the waiting time down to an acceptable level, and then the solutions in the longer term will be important to keep the deadlines to a greater extent than today. In order to get a real strengthening of the judicial hearing capacity in the police, we provide good training capacity. Police officers must also ensure that the training places at the Police College are filled up. The police units that have these issues need to be strengthened and made more robust, so that notifications can be made as soon as possible. Police districts are on commission from the Police Directorate to establish several assault teams locally. This will strengthen the priority of work on these kinds of issues. Some police districts have already implemented measures that point in the right direction, such as: Oslo Police Station, in cooperation with Oslo District Court, has launched new procedures for the denunciation of judicial hearings. The new procedures involve those responsible for the prosecution and investigation of a case, who are also responsible for the screening, coordination and quality follow-up of investigations in the case. In addition, a separate judicial hearing office has been set up in family violence cases at the Stovner Police Station. Oslo District Court has also changed the routines, by creating a new plan for this kind of thing. A more firm approach to the appointment of judges may reduce the time spent. Oslo police districts have also used wage resources, B-table, to motivate police officers and women to take longer to work on these kinds of issues. Experience with these measures so far seems promising. The waiting time at Oslo Police District has fallen considerably. There may be reason to consider whether both Oslo and elsewhere, where a number of measures have been taken, can form patterns for the rest of the country. Finally, I would like to stress that despite the difficulties and challenges encountered in the fight against abuse and violence against children, children's homes make a significant, positive effort for these vulnerable children. This applies not only to the hearings but also to the follow-up of the children and the support and assistance of their relatives. We're going to continue this positive work. I would also like to make it clear that there has been an important prerequisite for the optimal functioning of children's homes, that there is sufficient medical expertise with the necessary specialisation available in children's homes. It's a challenge for several of the children's homes. It is also a challenge because these are burdensome issues to work with, including for the medical profession. However, it is important that that competence is ensured. Last night I had a meeting with the Minister of Health and Care about this particular issue to ensure that we can find a model that will ensure that this competence is guaranteed for use in children's homes in the future. It's about resource use, but it's also about skills enhancement and competence development, because it's a very special kind of medical competence you need when you're going to do the research that's done here.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e4150	Thank you to the representative, Ropstad, who addresses this important interpellation, and to the Minister for the Answer. I am very pleased that the Right Party government is taking this issue seriously and is making good progress in the treatment of the new regulations for children's homes. Children who are subjected to violence and sexual abuse often experience the absence, lateness or need to be picked up from several different actors. In order to ensure better assistance and treatment for these children, the Norwegian government has set up a nationwide scheme of children’s homes. In children’s homes, interrogations, examinations and treatment take place at the same place. A good competence has subsequently been established among those conducting these hearings, which is important in order to ensure a good process when dealing with the cases in the judicial system. The establishment of children’s homes is at the root of a long-standing concern among professionals and others that children who are subjected to violence and abuse have met a fragmented and unbridled service and that there has been a risk that their children and their families will be subjected to additional stress in a situation that is initially difficult to deal with. The idea behind children’s homes is that children who are subjected to violence and sexual assault should be guaranteed a good and comprehensive assistance and treatment provision co-located in one place. Therefore, children’s homes are equipped for court hearings, medical examination and discussion and therapy. Children’s homes have introduced new methods of interrogation and medical examination that are less burdensome to children. A multidisciplinary team of staff helps the child to avoid being carried around from one instance to another to tell his or her history over and over again. Earlier this year, I attended a conference directed by the Right Party, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left, where violence in close relations was discussed. In the course of this seminar, a report was also presented to show that the extent of violence and abuse in close relations has unfortunately not fallen – despite the fact that there has been a great deal of political attention to increase the detection of violence and child abuse. But there has been a significant increase in the number of child questionings in recent years. For example, Oslo Police District, where 94 hearings were conducted in 2009, was conducted, while 208 court hearings were conducted in 2011. In addition, 16 police interviews were conducted at the children's home. Behind these figures we know that there are many vulnerable souls — children who need all the help and support they can get to get through what they have experienced. I want to use Paal-André Grinderud's mention of these little children from a TV interview yesterday as little princes and princesses. Over the years, there have been different practices in the use of children’s homes. The report of the working group, led by the current police officer in Troms, Ole Bredrup Säeverud, was handed over to the Ministry of Justice in October 2012. This is a very comprehensive report in which several elements are reviewed. Through this report, a view has been taken of the contribution and role of the judge, the appointment of the defender and the warning of sighted persons, the observation system, the deadline for the conduct of hearings, equality of morality and other issues of child abuse and mental retardation, who should be present during the hearings, where the hearings should be conducted, the use of remote meeting technology and the printing of the hearing. The report on the new regulations has been out for consultation and, as we have heard earlier today, had a deadline for consultation in April last year. These little princes and our princesses deserve us to get the new regulations in place as soon as possible, and thus achieve the intention of the State Children's House – and not least make the necessary clarifications to ensure that the hearings are conducted within the time limit given by law. To the right, it is an important principle that police interviews are conducted by specialised police investigators, and I therefore believe it is natural that the responsibility for the State Children's House lies with the police in the future. I look forward to the matter being dealt with in Stortinget, and to putting in place the new national regulations.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e4302	I hope that with this interpellation debate, we can raise a problem that is very relevant to the police today, especially in the larger cities. One constantly hears through the media that criminals who get out of prison disappear, and that people who don't have a permanent residence move freely around society without the authorities having control of them. The question is how we can have the most effective criminal justice care based on today's legislation. How can today’s situation tell us about how matters should be handled, as opposed to what the situation was like ten years ago? And what new grip should we and can we take? Basic legal certainty is a principle that is well anchored in Norway. Article 7 of the European Convention on Human Rights sets out the framework for dealing with cases in criminal matters. These are basic values that we should never deviate from, nor is it my intention to record today. The goal is to make the most possible while a person is being apprehended, in compliance with the criminal law § 183. This is both in the interests of society and in the interests of its people. Some people without a permanent residence or any other connection to the kingdom do criminal acts while they are in the country. When the police detect crime being committed by these groups, it is important that a tool be given to quickly determine the criminal cases. These criminal measures are often not serious enough to allow prosecution authorities to demand the perpetrators to remain in custody until the main proceedings, due to low levels of penalties. When they intend not to recognise the relationship, Section 248 of the Criminal Code is not applicable. Then the main negotiation is the only possibility that needs to be put right, with both disciplines and laymen. Today, one has deadlines for the main negotiation of a maximum of six weeks if the person is in custody, or the case is to be regarded as a matter of time, and three months in other cases. In practice, this has the consequence that the group of lawbreakers we are now talking about is released into the street after being apprehended. When the police are going to conduct the main hearings in the district court, they have to sue the votes, but then one no longer gets hold of them. They're wanted, but one thing isn't going to track them down, and their cases are going to lie in the drawers of the police. Police, courts and others then spend a disproportionate amount of time and resources trying to find these people in order to get the lawsuits carried out. The situation is often complete in the field, the evidence situation is clear, and only correction is lacking for the courts to decide on the issue of guilt and punishment. As an illustrative example of this type of crime, when it comes to first attempts at the sale of less drugs, the Norwegian Court of Justice in 2010, page 449, has ruled that the punishment should be imposed in prison for 18 days. Precautionary punishment, therefore, means that one cannot take care of them. For Oslo police districts, this has been a major problem in recent years. For a long time, it has been quite remarkable to fight open-source drug trafficking in the centre. To deal with this problem, it's getting rid of the sellers and the repeaters completely centrally. In this it is that criminal cases must be decided quickly. Now, not the cases where people are being attacked with drugs or where they are being observed when buying and selling drugs, are the most demanding of law enforcement, but with the level of penalties we have for some types of crime, it is important that matters are decided quickly. The police have long been eager to put in place a system in which a person can expedite the simpler criminal proceedings quickly. In accordance with Section 183 of the Criminal Procedure Act, the police are given permission to hold people apprehended for a certain period of time if the conditions for their presence exist. If we could have established arrangements in the courts where it was possible to conduct the main negotiations within the 72-hour deadline, it would have been very effective. The things that are currently in the drawers of the police houses could instead have been decided quickly. So one might ask himself the question if this would have gone beyond legal certainty. It's not the difficult and complicated things that this is all about. These are matters that have been solved in the city and which are proven to be entirely elaborate, typical winnings, minor drug law offences and the like. We are not talking about power cases, electoral and murder cases. Of course, this raises the question of resources. The courts must have free capacity in order to be able to receive such speedy matters quickly, and the prosecution authorities must be able to deal with the actor. But this is possible to solve, and everyone wants to expedite things quickly. It's important that we take over the new time we're living in, the crime scene is changing, and then it might make sense to think about something new. Effective criminal proceedings are important both for the accused and for any offended parties. Criminal cases that can't be decided because they can't get access to the perpetrator are just plugs in the system. Another major practical problem is the case where a voter gets people to a future main debate while in the police detention centre, and then an unannounced sentence is given to them afterwards because they do not attend the main hearing in the district court. Thus, the judgment is not served, and they must be demanded to be served. This, too, is a resource-demanding and inappropriate use of time. The criminal services that are to execute the judgment are also not allowed to do what they are to do. An arrangement in which one could be judged within the 72 hours of being able to sit in the arrest would also have solved this problem for those cases where it would not be defined as disproportionate to sit under arrest, cf. Law of Criminal Procedure § 170a. Putting them in place is central. A final judgment may be used as a ground for expulsion. It is important that Norway has an effective system when it comes to this, within the framework of our international commitments. When I call for quick courts, I don't mean to create new organs. I think we should give a clear signal that today's law should be used, as it allows for effective criminal proceedings. However, it is important that the right to free choice of defenders is not weakened, but that a good and appropriate solution is found. I so hope that the Minister of Justice agrees with this approach, and that he will take it up in the own forums, including the National Attorney and the Police Directorate, to raise awareness of these issues.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-04-03.ana.ud2238e4341	I want to start by thanking the interpellant for the opportunity to discuss a more effective criminal case chain. It's a very important theme that I'm very concerned about. The Interpellation calls for a rapid court for simpler criminal proceedings, where the perpetrator is a foreign national and without a permanent residence in Norway. Based on the context of what the representative now said in his statement, I perceive that she is not proposing to set up new courts, but the organisation of a rapid track within the existing court system for these cases. Before commenting on this in detail, I need to say a little bit about the development of the crime scene in relation to the group of criminals foreigners, and give a brief insight into the strategies and objectives of the government to streamline the criminal justice chain for foreigners who commit crime in Norway. The number of foreign citizens committing criminal acts has been increasing in recent years. There is reason to believe that this development will continue, for example: as a result of increased globalisation and mobility. In particular, Norway, as a rich country with a high standard of living, will be seen as attractive to criminals who live for a shorter or longer period of time. In order to achieve an effective fight against crime, it is important that the Norwegian authorities send out a clear signal that foreign nationals who commit crimes that qualify for expulsion are immediately given a decision on expulsion and are promptly sent out by Norway. In order to achieve this, there has to be good flow in that part of the chain involving police and judicial action, investigation and proceedings relating to the review and settlement of cases before the courts. I also mention that I am concerned about the next stages in the chain, which consists of a judicial verdict, accompanied by an expulsion decision and a quick return. Here's a whole context that we shouldn't lose sight of. The starting point for good business in the chain depends on the good practice the police have in carrying out a quick investigation, or an immediate investigation into simpler matters, and how effective we have in order to have the case settled quickly in the court. In addition, we know that a major challenge in these cases is that criminal discharges often try to avoid correction of the criminal case. Similarly also applies to the next joints in the chain. Here we see that attempts to make themselves inaccessible in order to prevent the announcement of a pending decision on expulsion and the subsequent efficiencies of the shipment itself are not uncommon. A kind of preliminary summary is that effective criminal proceedings in this context result in an objective of establishing mechanisms that both ensure rapid judgment and prevent avoidance. From this point of view, I am continuing through the possibilities and limitations of the existing legislation in order to achieve effective criminal proceedings on these matters. A review of the effectiveness of current legislation should be carried out on the basis of the penalties to be applied. There are roughly three main groups here. One will be issues where the reaction is fine, the other where the reaction is conditional imprisonment, or short, unconditional sentences, and the third case that qualifies for longer, unconditional imprisonment, estimated from 45 days onwards. For the Botgroup, the cases could be decided by a bill issued by the police. Further flow in the chain depends on good cooperation procedures between the police and the UDI, so that expulsion decisions can be taken immediately. I mention that last year, about summer time, access to expulsion was extended to conditions determined by fines, including Nazirite. As a result, the number of this group has increased slightly and the efficiency requirement is therefore even more important than in the past. At the other end of the scale, we have cases that qualify for unconditional imprisonment over 45 days. In these cases, there will often be a risk of avoidance that allows access to custody. It follows from Section 275 of the Criminal Procedures Act that the main proceedings in matters of custody should be initiated within six weeks of the matter being brought before the District Court. This provision does not have a shorter period for foreign nationals. If the defendant confesses before the first prison meeting, I have been informed that several District Courts, in agreement with the prosecution authority, are practising the verdict of the confession case in the prison meeting. This practice is very distinct and shows the possibilities for improving efficiency within the current framework. In the middle group, we find cases of short, unconditional sentences under 45 days, cases ending in conditional prison, and proceedings of fines brought before the court, because the purpose did not decide. Many of these cases often have a simple and transparent picture of evidence. For the short unconditional punishments, one will be able to be judged by confession in prison meetings, as I have already pointed out. However, if the case requires a main negotiation, or the expected penalty is fine or conditional imprisonment, the risk of over-sunification will normally prevent custody imprisonment, because retention is not carried out properly enough. There's this group of criminal foreigners we're left with here in the middle group, which is really the background of this interpellation -- as I see it. I would add: it is this group that is particularly challenging for the effectiveness of the criminal action chain. I am very concerned about this issue, and I have therefore asked the Police Directorate to obtain information to enumerate the group and the possible extent of criminal acts the group commits. The collection of information is currently limited to the districts of Oslo, Asker, Bærum and Romerike. I have also asked that work be started on the changes in the law that are needed to follow up the interesting recording by the scapegoat writer in Oslo District Court in the evening post yesterday, where he sets out a quick track for foreign criminals. I share his assessment that today's system creates unnecessary resource use. The author's willingness to think again promises well for the further work on this matter. What kind of concrete legislative changes it can be about, we're going to get closer to. The requirement for speed and efficiency will determine the nature of the legislative changes involved and the extent to which changes need to be made in each provision. This can be illustrated in more detail by the concrete proposal of the inter-pellant. The ambition of the proposal is that judgement should be made within the time limit laid down in Section 183 of the Criminal Procedures Act, that is to say, preferably the day following the inception or at the latest on the third day after the inception. The timeframe makes the proposal difficult to reconcile with some key provisions of process legislation in terms of practical implementation. One example is the requirement of three days' notice before the main negotiation, on which the interpellant himself had the right to the right of the defence election, the right of the defence to have time to prepare the case and discuss it with a view to interpreting, drawing with judges to the main negotiation and the rules on appeal periods and appeal proceedings. Of course, changes in existing legislation will need to look at the organisation of the courts in question and the new procedures that need to be developed between the police, the courts and the defence. The experience of Oslo’s rights related to the so-called 518 model shows that actors contribute to better efficiency when schemes are organised for it. In short, courtroom 518 in Oslo has been earmarked for simpler criminal cases of half a day's duration. The General Court also has an agreement with the Oslo police district, which means that the police itself blames the main debate in this Chamber. When e.g. the victim is without a domicile, the police will be able to legally sue him for the main proceedings and to be convicted of the criminal offence. Oslo District Court ruled 192 such cases in 2012 and 159 cases in 2013, indicating how much efficiency gains such schemes can generate. The government statement also says that we should investigate the rapid court in a larger context and that it should be thoroughly investigated. Of course we'll come back to that. In summary, the government shares the inter-pellant's wish to have more effective handling of these cases, and we will come back to what relevant changes in legislation are needed to implement them.
